              - generic [ref=e100]:

          - region "Article content" [ref=e102]:
            - generic [ref=e103]:
              - generic [ref=e104]:
                - heading "Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e105]: Abstract
                - paragraph [ref=e106]: Accumulated evidence strongly indicates that oxidative stress, characterized by an imbalance between reactive oxygen species (ROS) production and antioxidants in favor of oxidants, plays an important role in disease pathogenesis. However, ROS can act as signaling molecules and fulfill essential physiological functions at basal levels. Each ROS would be different in the extent to stimulate and contribute to different pathophysiological effects. Importantly, multiple ROS generators can be activated either concomitantly or sequentially by relevant signaling molecules for redox biological functions. Here, we summarized the current knowledge related to chemical and biochemical features of primary ROS species and corresponding antioxidants. Metabolic pathways of five major ROS generators and five ROS clearance systems were described, including their ROS products, specific ROS enriched tissue, cell and organelle, and relevant functional implications. We provided an overview of ROS generation and induction at different levels of metabolism. We classified 11 ROS species into three types based on their reactivity and target selectivity and presented ROS homeostasis and functional implications in pathological and physiological status. This article intensively reviewed and refined biochemical basis, metabolic signaling and regulation, functional insights, and provided guidance for the identification of novel therapeutic targets.
                - paragraph [ref=e108]:
                  - strong [ref=e109]: "Keywords:"
                  - text: Redox regulation and homeostasis, ROS generators, Antioxidants
              - generic [ref=e110]:
                - heading "1. Introduction" [level=2] [ref=e111]: 1. Introduction
                - paragraph [ref=e112]:
                  - text: Oxidative stress, an imbalance between reactive oxygen species (ROS) production and antioxidants in favor of oxidants leading to cell damages [
                  - link "1" [ref=e113] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib1"
                  - text: "], is an important feature of cardiovascular disease (CVD) and other diseases. Case-control studies showed a strong link between increased oxidative stress biomarkers, such as F2-isoprostanes and 8-epi-PGF2α ["
                  - link "2" [ref=e114] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib2"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "3" [ref=e115] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib3"
                  - text: "], and CVD ["
                  - link "4" [ref=e116] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib4"
                  - text: "] (atherosclerosis ["
                  - link "5" [ref=e117] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib5"
                  - text: "], myocardial infarction ["
                  - link "6" [ref=e118] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib6"
                  - text: "], heart failure, and stroke ["
                  - link "7" [ref=e119] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib7"
                  - text: "]), metabolic disorders (obesity ["
                  - link "8" [ref=e120] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib8"
                  - text: "], diabetes ["
                  - link "8" [ref=e121] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib8"
                  - text: "], hyperlipidemia ["
                  - link "9" [ref=e122] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib9"
                  - text: "], hyperhomocysteinemia (HHcy) ["
                  - link "10" [ref=e123] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib10"
                  - text: "]) and cancer ["
                  - link "11" [ref=e124] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib11"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "12" [ref=e125] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib12"
                  - text: "]. Cohort studies found that high intake of antioxidants, such as vitamin A, C, and E, was associated with a lower rate of CVD ["
                  - link "13" [ref=e126] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib13"
                  - text: "] and cancer ["
                  - link "14" [ref=e127] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib14"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "15" [ref=e128] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib15"
                  - text: "], although the majority of large scale clinical trials yielded negative results for oral antioxidant supplementation ["
                  - link "16" [ref=e129] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib16"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "17" [ref=e130] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib17"
                  - text: "]. Moreover, experimental studies provided considerable evidence supporting the hypothesis that oxidative stress was the common mechanism for cancer ["
                  - link "18" [ref=e131] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib18"
                  - text: "] and CVD ["
                  - link "19" [ref=e132] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib19"
                  - text: "]. In addition, inhibiting ROS-generating enzymes using pharmacologic or genetic approaches reduced ROS levels and disease phenotype in experiment disease models ["
                  - link "[20]" [ref=e133] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib20"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[21]" [ref=e134] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib21"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[22]" [ref=e135] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib22"
                  - text: "]. However, the negative findings in clinical trials may be due to three major reasons. Firstly, the targeted participants in clinical trials might not be as specific as models used in preclinical studies ["
                  - link "16" [ref=e136] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib16"
                  - text: "]. For example, the index disease would be concomitant with other disorders due to recruiting broad study populations into a trial. Secondly, the excessive ROS might be inadequate to be removed ["
                  - link "16" [ref=e137] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib16"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "17" [ref=e138] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib17"
                  - text: "]. It has been reported that the rate constant between general scavengers with ROS is lower than the rate constants between ROS-targeted biomolecules with ROS. Nitric oxide (NO"
                  - superscript [ref=e139]: •
                  - text: ) is a targeted biomolecule of superoxide radical anion (O
                  - subscript [ref=e140]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e141]: •-
                  - text: ). The rate constant between vitamin C with O
                  - subscript [ref=e142]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e143]: •-
                  - text: is 10
                  - superscript [ref=e144]: "5"
                  - text: times lower than the rate constant between NO
                  - superscript [ref=e145]: •
                  - text: with O
                  - subscript [ref=e146]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e147]: •- 23
                  - text: . The required concentration of vitamin C within the cell needs to be extremely high in order to compete with O
                  - subscript [ref=e148]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e149]: •-
                  - text: . Finally, conventional antioxidants lack the specificity of sites or target [
                  - link "17" [ref=e150] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib17"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "24" [ref=e151] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib24"
                  - text: "], which indiscriminately interferes with a number of ROS protective processes. ROS act as signaling molecules involving cell survival and death via modulation of transcription factors and epigenetic pathways. The physiological level of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e152]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e153]: "2"
                  - text: is essential to regulate basic biological processes such as cell proliferation/differentiation and metabolic homeostasis [
                  - link "25" [ref=e154] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib25"
                  - text: "]. General antioxidants may reduce ROS levels to below “normal range”. Additionally, redox signaling also is associated with time-dependent ROS metabolism process and organelle function, that is, the spatiotemporal differentiation of Redox ["
                  - link "26" [ref=e155] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib26"
                  - text: "]. Therefore, it is in great need to better understand the biochemical and metabolic regulation of ROS homeostasis and to develop more specific and effective strategies for oxidative stress in human disease. A future therapeutic strategy would be to modulate the oxidative stress without severely disturbing general redox homeostasis and less harmful to healthy tissues/cells."

                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e275] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/tbl1/
                  - paragraph [ref=e278]:
                    - text: ROS are a chemically reactive species containing oxygen which is classified to free radical and non-radical species. The table describes chemical features of major ROS including reactivity, lifetime, diffusion distance, preferred target, and measurement methodology. Reactivity is defined according to one electron reduction potential (E
                    - superscript [ref=e279]: o
                    - text: ) at pH7, or the reaction rate with glutathione (GSH) for radicals and non-radicals, and provided with a relative description of + and ++ (Low), +++ (mediate), and ≥ ++++ (high). Lifetime equals the natural log2 divided by the sum of the products of rate constant and concentrations for all molecules that ROS react with. Diffusion distance is calculated by using the formula x = (6Dt)
                    - superscript [ref=e280]: 1/2
                    - text: (x, D, t stand for diffusion distance, diffusion coefficients and lifetime, respectively).
                    - strong [ref=e281]: "Abbreviation:"
                    - text: Cys, Cysteine; DAF-2DA, 5-diaminofluorescein diacetate; DHE, dihydroethidium; DMPO, 5,5-dimethylpyrroline-N-oxide; EPR, Electron paramagnetic resonance; Fe-DETC, Iron-diethyldithiocarbamate; Fe–S, Iron (II) sulfide; GC-MS, Gas chromatography-mass spectrometry; HPLC, High performance liquid chromatography; Met, Methionine; PG1, Peroxygreen 1; PC1,Peroxycrimson 1; ROS, Reactive oxygen species; TMPyP, 5,10,15,20-tetrakis(N-methyl-4-pyridyl)-21H, 23H-porphine; Tyr, Tyrosine; Trp, Tryptophan; NA, Not Available. Unpaired electron denoted by a superscript dot to the right preceding the charge symbol.
                - paragraph [ref=e282]:
                  - strong [ref=e283]: ROS
                  - text: is a term for chemically reactive species containing oxygen. Another term such as reactive nitrogen species (RNS) was used for ROS containing nitrogen. Because ROS and RNS are closely interrelated, we use “ROS” as the general term representing both. Basing on their chemical features, ROS can be divided into two classes, free radicals, and non-radicals (
                  - link "Table 1" [ref=e284] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tbl1"
                  - text: ). The free radicals usually refer to ROS containing one unpaired reactive electron in the outer orbit and belong to one-electron oxidants. The major free radicals include nitric oxide (NO
                  - superscript [ref=e285]: •
                  - text: ), superoxide radical anion (O
                  - subscript [ref=e286]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e287]: •-
                  - text: ), hydroxyl radical (OH
                  - superscript [ref=e288]: •)
                  - text: ", carbonate radical anion (CO"
                  - subscript [ref=e289]: "3"
                  - superscript [ref=e290]: •-
                  - text: ), nitrogen dioxide (NO
                  - subscript [ref=e291]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e292]: •
                  - text: ), and alkoxyl/alkyl peroxyl (RO
                  - superscript [ref=e293]: •
                  - text: /ROO
                  - superscript [ref=e294]: •
                  - text: ). The non-free radicals do not contain unpaired electron and belong to two-electron oxidants. Hydrogen peroxide (H
                  - subscript [ref=e295]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e296]: "2"
                  - text: ), peroxynitrite (ONOO
                  - superscript [ref=e297]: −
                  - text: )/peroxynitrous acid (ONOOH), and hypochlorous acid (HOCl) are the major non-radical species. The presence of unpaired electron confers to some shared properties among free radicals that are distinct from non-radicals. In general, free radicals are more unstable and reactive than non-radicals. Their reactions with targeted molecules favor a chain-reacting behavior, which consists of an initiation step, a propagation step, and a termination step. Typically, the initiation step is the generation of starting free radicals from initiators via thermal, radiation or electron transfer (redox) processes. Within cells, usually, oxygen (O
                  - subscript [ref=e298]: "2"
                  - text: ) is the initiator. The electron transfer process catalyzed by various endogenous ROS generators accounts for the production of starting radicals. There is a net increase of free radical in this step. Once a starting radical is formed, it can react with a stable molecule without unpaired electrons to form a covalent bond. The targeted molecule then loses an electron and becomes the new radical species. This process is called propagation in which there is no net increase of free radicals. This propagation continues until the radicals react with phenolic antioxidants such as vitamin E, flavonoids and ascorbic acid, or with another free radical to form a covalent bond, a step called termination which leads to a net decrease of free radicals. Relative reactivity of free radicals can be simply ranked according to the one-electron reduction potential (E
                  - superscript [ref=e299]:
                    - emphasis [ref=e300]: O
                  - text: ) which reflect the tendency of a chemical species to receive electrons and to be reduced [
                  - link "27" [ref=e301] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib27"
                  - text: "] and is graded in the range from one plus (+) to five plus in"
                  - link "Table 1" [ref=e302] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tbl1"
                  - text: . Relative reactivity of non-radical is based on their second order rate constant (
                  - emphasis [ref=e303]: k
                  - text: ), a chemical kinetic parameter for reaction rate with glutathione (GSH) [
                  - link "27" [ref=e304] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib27"
                  - text: "]. As indicated in"
                  - link "Table 1" [ref=e305] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tbl1"
                  - text: ", the lifetime of ROS ranges from 10"
                  - superscript [ref=e306]: −9
                  - text: to 1 s, and diffusion distance ranges from 10
                  - superscript [ref=e307]: −12
                  - text: to 10 m. ROS can target a large scale of molecules, including protein residues (Cys, Met, Tyr, and Trp), Fe–S, ROS and any macromolecules (DNA, RNA, protein, and lipids), which leads to various pathological changes. The major analytical approaches to measure ROS are spectroscopic and electrochemical methods, which were described in highly comprehensive reviews [
                  - link "[28]" [ref=e308] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib28"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[29]" [ref=e309] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib29"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[30]" [ref=e310] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib30"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[31]" [ref=e311] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib31"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[32]" [ref=e312] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib32"
                  - text: "]. Spectroscopic methods include direct measurement of adducts formed between ROS and endogenous target molecules (mass spectrometry), exogenous fluorescent probe (fluorescence) or spin traps (electron paramagnetic resonance spectroscopy), and indirect measurement of byproducts formed between ROS and other chemical species (chemiluminescence). Electrochemical methods directly measure ROS using selective biosensors and are technically challenging."
                - generic [ref=e313]:
                  - heading "2.1. Radicals" [level=3] [ref=e314]
                  - paragraph [ref=e315]:
                    - strong [ref=e316]: "NO"
                    - superscript [ref=e317]:
                      - strong [ref=e318]: •
                    - text: — NO
                    - superscript [ref=e319]: •
                    - text: is a very weak oxidant with the E
                    - superscript [ref=e320]:
                      - emphasis [ref=e321]: O
                    - text: ∼0.80 V, with the longest life among identified ROS (∼1s). It has high solubility, being able to diffuse across a membrane freely up to 10 m. This property makes it difficult to define its cellular origin because the detected NO
                    - superscript [ref=e322]: •
                    - text: can be from another cell or even different tissues. NO
                    - superscript [ref=e323]: •
                    - text: tends to be oxidized instead of to be reduced and is relatively inert to most biomolecules. It prefers reacting with free radicals and metals. Reaction with different free radicals results in the different biological effect. When it reacts with the lipid-free radicals such as lipid oxy (LO
                    - superscript [ref=e324]: •
                    - text: ) or peroxyl radical (LOO
                    - superscript [ref=e325]: •
                    - text: ), LONO/LOONO will be formed and the lipid peroxidation will be terminated (protective). When it reacts with O
                    - subscript [ref=e326]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e327]: •-
                    - text: ", more potent oxidant ONOO"
                    - superscript [ref=e328]: −
                    - text: will be generated and oxidative stress is induced (deleterious). When it reacts with protein radicals, a nitrosylated protein will be generated. NO
                    - superscript [ref=e329]: •
                    - text: targets on metals (heme iron, non-heme iron, Fe–S cluster, and copper compound) and results in nitrosyl-metal complex formation, metal oxidation, and metal reduction [
                    - link "33" [ref=e330] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib33"
                    - text: "]. The best example of a nitrosyl-metal complex is the nitrosyl-heme complex within the soluble guanylate cyclase (sGC), leading to the alternation of sGC configuration and subsequent sGC activation. Activated sGC then converts guanosine triphosphate (GTP) to 3′, 5′-cyclic guanosine monophosphate (cGMP) leading to vessel relaxation ["
                    - link "34" [ref=e331] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib34"
                    - text: "]. The nitrosyl-heme complex formation has also been reported in mediating the reversible inhibition of cytochrome P450, catalase (CAT), lipoxygenase (LPO), myeloperoxidase (MPO) and nitric oxide synthase (NOS) ["
                    - link "35" [ref=e332] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib35"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "36" [ref=e333] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib36"
                    - text: "]. NO"
                    - superscript [ref=e334]: •
                    - text: is commonly detected by using fluorescent probe 4,5-diaminofluorescein diacetate (DAF-2 DA) [
                    - link "37" [ref=e335] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib37"
                    - text: "] or spin trap iron diethyldithiocarbamate (Fe-DETC) ["
                    - link "38" [ref=e336] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib38"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e337]:
                    - strong [ref=e338]: O
                    - subscript [ref=e339]:
                      - strong [ref=e340]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e341]:
                      - strong [ref=e342]: •-
                    - text: — O
                    - subscript [ref=e343]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e344]: •-
                    - text: is also a weak radical (E
                    - superscript [ref=e345]:
                      - emphasis [ref=e346]: O
                    - text: ∼ 0.94 V). This is in part due to its anionic charge that prevents its access to electron-rich centers. It has a relatively short lifetime (∼50 ms) because it can be rapidly dismutated to H
                    - subscript [ref=e347]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e348]: "2"
                    - text: spontaneously or by superoxide dismutase (SOD). The anionic charge limits its diffusion distance at ∼320 nm O
                    - subscript [ref=e349]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e350]: •-
                    - text: prefers to react with NO
                    - superscript [ref=e351]: •
                    - text: or the transition metals particularly the Fe–S cluster. When it reacts with NO
                    - superscript [ref=e352]: •
                    - text: ", because of the much shorter lifetime and diffusion distance than NO"
                    - superscript [ref=e353]: •
                    - text: ", the reaction mostly occurs at the site of O"
                    - subscript [ref=e354]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e355]: •-
                    - text: generation. When it reacts with Fe–S clusters, Fe–S clusters will be oxidized into unstable high valent clusters which decompose and then releases free Fe
                    - superscript [ref=e356]: 2+
                    - text: . For example, the SoxR, a transcriptional factor in
                    - emphasis [ref=e357]: E. coli
                    - text: to regulate resistance genes to prevent oxidative damage, is active only when its Fe–S clusters is oxidized by O
                    - subscript [ref=e358]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e359]: •-
                    - text: to high valent cluster [
                    - link "39" [ref=e360] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib39"
                    - text: "]. O"
                    - subscript [ref=e361]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e362]: •-
                    - text: is usually detected by fluorescent probe dihydroethidium (DHE) or spin trap 5,5-dimethylpyrroline-N-oxide (DMPO) [
                    - link "30" [ref=e363] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib30"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e364]:
                    - strong [ref=e365]: OH
                    - superscript [ref=e366]:
                      - strong [ref=e367]: •
                    - text: — OH
                    - superscript [ref=e368]: •
                    - text: is the most reactive radical (E
                    - superscript [ref=e369]:
                      - emphasis [ref=e370]: O
                    - text: ∼2.31 V). It can indiscriminately react with most of the biomolecules including lipids, proteins, carbohydrates, and DNA, at a rate close to diffusion limit. Because of its extremely high reactivity and ability to rapidly react with a target at its site of formation, it has a very short lifetime at ∼10
                    - superscript [ref=e371]: −9
                    - text: s and the shortest diffusion distance at ∼4.5 nm. OH
                    - superscript [ref=e372]: •
                    - text: reacts with its targets through hydrogen (H) abstraction, addition, and electron transfer mechanism. OH
                    - superscript [ref=e373]: •
                    - text: can easily attract the H atoms from organic compounds (proteins and lipids) particularly those with weak bond, generating a carbon center free radical (R
                    - superscript [ref=e374]: •
                    - text: ). Isoprostanes are also produced through hydroxyl radical attack on arachidonic acid and are used as clinical markers of oxidative stress for various conditions [
                    - link "40" [ref=e375] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib40"
                    - text: "]. For the aromatic compounds (DNA/RNA), OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e376]: •
                    - text: can be added directly to produce hydroxylated radical adducts. These stable adducts such as 8-Oxo-deoxyguanosine [
                    - link "41" [ref=e377] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib41"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "42" [ref=e378] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib42"
                    - text: "] can be detected at extremely low concentration and used as a biomarker of OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e379]: •
                    - text: . When reacting with anions, OH
                    - superscript [ref=e380]: •
                    - text: takes an electron, producing a corresponding new radical. Hydroxylated radical adducts can be detected by HPLC electrochemical (HPLC-EC) detector and by GC-MS analysis of trimethylsilyl derivatives [
                    - link "43" [ref=e381] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib43"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e382]:
                    - strong [ref=e383]: CO
                    - subscript [ref=e384]:
                      - strong [ref=e385]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e386]:
                      - strong [ref=e387]: •-
                    - text: — CO
                    - subscript [ref=e388]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e389]: •-
                    - text: is a highly reactive radical (E
                    - superscript [ref=e390]:
                      - emphasis [ref=e391]: O
                    - text: ∼1.78 V) with a very short lifetime (∼10
                    - superscript [ref=e392]: −6
                    - text: s) and very short diffusion distance at ∼152 nm CO
                    - subscript [ref=e393]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e394]: •-
                    - text: can oxidize biothiols, metalloproteins, protein residues (Trp, Cys, Tyr, Met, and histidine) and guanine moieties of DNA/RNA to the corresponding radicals [
                    - link "44" [ref=e395] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib44"
                    - text: "]. CO3"
                    - superscript [ref=e396]: •-
                    - text: has been proposed as a key mediator of the ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e397]: −
                    - text: oxidative damage [
                    - link "45" [ref=e398] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib45"
                    - text: "]. Like OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e399]: •
                    - text: ", CO"
                    - subscript [ref=e400]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e401]: •-
                    - text: oxidizes these targets through H abstraction or electron transfer mechanism. Unlike OH
                    - superscript [ref=e402]: •
                    - text: ", CO"
                    - subscript [ref=e403]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e404]: •-
                    - text: oxidation might not produce or probably form radical adducts that have a short lifetime to be detected. Therefore, CO
                    - subscript [ref=e405]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e406]: •-
                    - text: cannot be measured by HPLC-EC detector or GC-MS analysis. CO
                    - subscript [ref=e407]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e408]: •-
                    - text: can be directly detected by continuous flow EPR, but requiring high instantaneous concentrations [
                    - link "46" [ref=e409] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib46"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e410]:
                    - strong [ref=e411]: "NO"
                    - subscript [ref=e412]:
                      - strong [ref=e413]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e414]:
                      - strong [ref=e415]: •
                    - text: — NO
                    - subscript [ref=e416]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e417]: •
                    - text: is a moderately reactive radical (E
                    - superscript [ref=e418]:
                      - emphasis [ref=e419]: O
                    - text: ∼1.04 V) and has short diffusion distance (∼188 nm). It reacts with its target predominantly through electron transfer mechanism [
                    - link "47" [ref=e420] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib47"
                    - text: "]. It oxidizes the thiols to thiyl free radicals, which subsequently react with NO"
                    - superscript [ref=e421]: •
                    - text: to generate nitrosothiol (RSNO). It can oxidize the tyrosine residue to tyrosyl radical, but the reaction is slower than its reaction with thiols. NO
                    - subscript [ref=e422]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e423]: •
                    - text: "-mediated tyrosine nitration is likely relying on pre-existing tyrosine free-radicals induced by CO"
                    - subscript [ref=e424]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e425]: •-
                    - text: . Biological nitration of tyrosine residues is also largely affected by the nanoenvironment of the tyrosine such as neighboring acidic residues (e.g. aspartate) that can cause partial abstraction of the proton of the phenolic OH-group in tyrosine [
                    - link "48" [ref=e426] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib48"
                    - text: "]. In addition, metal enzymatic catalysis also confers specificity for nitration of nearby tyrosine residues (eg. the nitration of tyrosine 430 in prostacyclin synthase ["
                    - link "49" [ref=e427] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib49"
                    - text: "])."
                  - paragraph [ref=e428]:
                    - strong [ref=e429]: RO
                    - superscript [ref=e430]:
                      - strong [ref=e431]: •
                    - strong [ref=e432]: /ROO
                    - superscript [ref=e433]: •
                    - text: — RO
                    - superscript [ref=e434]: •
                    - text: /ROO
                    - superscript [ref=e435]: •
                    - text: are moderately reactive radicals (E
                    - superscript [ref=e436]:
                      - emphasis [ref=e437]: O
                    - text: "> 1.78 V). Its reactivity can be affected by the substituents at the α-carbon. An electron-withdrawing group increases the reactivity while electron-donating groups decrease it. In lipid peroxidation, LO"
                    - superscript [ref=e438]: •
                    - text: /LOO
                    - superscript [ref=e439]: •
                    - text: can rapidly abstract the H from nearby lipid, generating new lipid free radicals and propagating the chain reaction. RO
                    - superscript [ref=e440]: •
                    - text: /ROO
                    - superscript [ref=e441]: •
                    - text: can be detected by lower temperature EPR [
                    - link "50" [ref=e442] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib50"
                    - text: "]. In addition, products of oxidative stress such as isoprostanes can be used for indirect detection RO"
                    - superscript [ref=e443]: •
                    - text: /ROO
                    - superscript [ref=e444]: •40
                    - text: .
                - generic [ref=e445]:
                  - heading "2.2. Non-radicals" [level=3] [ref=e446]
                  - paragraph [ref=e447]:
                    - strong [ref=e448]: H
                    - subscript [ref=e449]:
                      - strong [ref=e450]: "2"
                    - strong [ref=e451]: O
                    - subscript [ref=e452]:
                      - strong [ref=e453]: "2"
                    - text: – H
                    - subscript [ref=e454]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e455]: "2"
                    - text: is a mild non-radical with a relatively long lifetime (∼1 ms) and with a long diffusion distance at ∼1.5mm. H
                    - subscript [ref=e456]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e457]: "2"
                    - text: can react with Fe–S cluster, loosely bound metals, glutathione, free cysteine (Cys) and methionine (Met) residues but at a relatively slower rate than with Cys residues. Its reaction rate with Cys residue depends on the ionization state of Cys residue. Thiolate anion form (Cys–S-) of Cys residue favors a faster reaction than the protonated form (Cys–SH) of Cys residue. In physiological condition, Cys residue usually exists in the form of Cys–S-. Low levels of H
                    - subscript [ref=e458]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e459]: "2"
                    - text: can oxidize the Cys–S- to the sulfenic form (Cys–SOH). Cys–SOH can be reduced back to Cys–S- by thioredoxin (TRX) and GSH. High levels of H
                    - subscript [ref=e460]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e461]: "2"
                    - text: can further oxidize Cys–SOH to the irreversible form of sulfinic (Cys–SO
                    - subscript [ref=e462]: "2"
                    - text: H) or sulfonic (Cys–SO
                    - subscript [ref=e463]: "3"
                    - text: H). Despite of a fast reaction with Cys–S, its reactions with seleno, thiol or heme peroxidases are much faster. H
                    - subscript [ref=e464]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e465]: "2"
                    - text: can be quickly converted to H
                    - subscript [ref=e466]: "2"
                    - text: O by these surrounding peroxidases before reaching Cys–S-. Therefore, even though H
                    - subscript [ref=e467]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e468]: "2"
                    - text: is highly diffusible, it is usually restricted within a small range from its site of generation. This compartmentation allows the H
                    - subscript [ref=e469]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e470]: "2"
                    - text: to selectively target the thiols protein such as protein tyrosine phosphatases (PTPs) that are restricted within the same compartment. When H
                    - subscript [ref=e471]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e472]: "2"
                    - text: reacts with some heme peroxidases like MPO or reduced transition metal centers, more reactive free-radicals like OH
                    - superscript [ref=e473]: •
                    - text: or non-free-radicals like HOCl will be produced. The Amplex Red probe can be used to detect and quantitate H
                    - subscript [ref=e474]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e475]: "2"
                    - text: because of its high sensitivity [
                    - link "29" [ref=e476] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib29"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e477]:
                    - strong [ref=e478]: ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e479]:
                      - strong [ref=e480]: −
                    - text: — ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e481]: −
                    - text: is a highly reactive non-radical with a short lifetime (∼10–20 ms) [
                    - link "51" [ref=e482] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib51"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "52" [ref=e483] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib52"
                    - text: "] and a relatively long diffusion distance >50 μm (an average cellular length). ONOO"
                    - superscript [ref=e484]: −
                    - text: can react with its favored targets located in the same or adjacent cells. In most cases, ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e485]: −
                    - text: oxidizes a wide variety of biomolecules through its derivatives CO
                    - subscript [ref=e486]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e487]: •-
                    - text: ", NO"
                    - subscript [ref=e488]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e489]: •
                    - text: and OH
                    - superscript [ref=e490]: •
                    - text: generated in RNS pathway (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e491] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ). By its own, it can selectively react with few molecules including CO
                    - subscript [ref=e492]: "2"
                    - text: ", thiols, selenium compounds and metal centers. Reaction with CO"
                    - subscript [ref=e493]: "2"
                    - text: results in the generation of more toxic free-radicals CO
                    - subscript [ref=e494]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e495]: •-
                    - text: and NO
                    - subscript [ref=e496]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e497]: •
                    - text: (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e498] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ) which account for the most oxidative and nitrative effect of ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e499]: −
                    - text: . Reaction with thiols results in the generation of the corresponding disulfide. In contrast, its derivatives CO
                    - subscript [ref=e500]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e501]: •-
                    - text: and NO
                    - subscript [ref=e502]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e503]: •
                    - text: will oxidize thiol to thiyl free-radicals. Its reaction with the thiol peroxidase (PDRX) has the highest second-rate constants. This makes PDRX the most efficient ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e504]: −
                    - text: scavengers. Rapid reaction with selenium peroxidase (GPX) is another efficient way for scavenging ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e505]: −
                    - text: . Reaction with the metal centers can cause disruption of the active site and loss of enzyme activity. ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e506]: −
                    - text: can be directly detected by boronate-based probe [
                    - link "28" [ref=e507] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib28"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "30" [ref=e508] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib30"
                    - text: "]. ONOO"
                    - superscript [ref=e509]: −
                    - text: can be indirectly detected by measuring its biomarker 3-nitrotyrosine (3-NT) with HPLC/MS [
                    - link "53" [ref=e510] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib53"
                    - text: "]."
                  - figure [ref=e511]:
                    - heading "Fig. 1." [level=4] [ref=e512]
                    - paragraph [ref=e513]:
                      - link "Fig. 1 🔍" [ref=e514] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=6831867_gr1.jpg
                        - img "Fig. 1" [ref=e515]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e517] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/fig1/
                    - paragraph [ref=e519]:
                      - text: "Reactive oxygen species (ROS) metabolism. A. ROS generation: Cellular ROS is generated by 5 pathways. 1) Superoxide generation: O"
                      - subscript [ref=e520]: "2"
                      - superscript [ref=e521]: •-
                      - text: is derived from oxygen (O
                      - subscript [ref=e522]: "2"
                      - text: ") after receiving an electron from various oxidases or from mitochondrial electron transport chain (ETC). 2) Reactive nitrogen species generation: O"
                      - subscript [ref=e523]: "2"
                      - superscript [ref=e524]: •-
                      - text: reacts with nitric oxide (NO
                      - superscript [ref=e525]: •
                      - text: ) to form peroxynitrite (ONOO
                      - superscript [ref=e526]: −
                      - text: ). At physiological pH (pKa = 6.8), ONOO
                      - superscript [ref=e527]: −
                      - text: is in equilibrium with peroxynitrous acid (ONOOH). In the aqueous phase, ONOO
                      - superscript [ref=e528]: −
                      - text: rapidly reacts with carbon dioxide (CO
                      - subscript [ref=e529]: "2"
                      - text: ) to generate carbonate radical anion (CO
                      - subscript [ref=e530]: "3"
                      - superscript [ref=e531]: •-
                      - text: ) and nitrogen dioxide (NO
                      - subscript [ref=e532]: "2"
                      - superscript [ref=e533]: •
                      - text: ). In the hydrophobic phase, ONOOH can undergo homolytic fission to generate hydroxyl radical (OH
                      - superscript [ref=e534]: •-
                      - text: ) and NO
                      - subscript [ref=e535]: "2"
                      - superscript [ref=e536]: •
                      - text: ". 3) Hydrogen peroxide generation: O"
                      - subscript [ref=e537]: "2"
                      - superscript [ref=e538]: •-
                      - text: is dismutated spontaneously or by superoxide dismutase (SOD) into H
                      - subscript [ref=e539]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e540]: "2"
                      - text: . NOX4 and DOX1/2 can also dismutate O
                      - subscript [ref=e541]: "2"
                      - text: directly to H
                      - subscript [ref=e542]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e543]: "2"
                      - text: ". 4) Hydroxyl radical generation: OH"
                      - superscript [ref=e544]: •-
                      - text: can be derived from ONOOH as described in pathway 2. It can also be generated from H
                      - subscript [ref=e545]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e546]: "2"
                      - text: through Haber–Weiss reaction in the presence of transition metals, such as Fe
                      - superscript [ref=e547]: 2+
                      - text: ", Cu"
                      - superscript [ref=e548]: 2+
                      - text: ", Zn"
                      - superscript [ref=e549]: 2+
                      - text: . H
                      - subscript [ref=e550]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e551]: "2"
                      - text: can be converted to hypochlorous acid (HOCl) by myeloperoxidase (MPO), and then react with O
                      - subscript [ref=e552]: "2"
                      - superscript [ref=e553]: •-
                      - text: to form OH
                      - superscript [ref=e554]: •
                      - text: ". 5) Lipid radical generation: Lipid peroxidation is usually initiated by OH"
                      - superscript [ref=e555]: •-
                      - text: abstracting an allylic hydrogen (H) from unsaturated lipid and forming the carbon-centered lipid radical (L
                      - superscript [ref=e556]: •
                      - text: ). Generated L
                      - superscript [ref=e557]: •
                      - text: then rapidly reacts with O [
                      - link "2" [ref=e558] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bib2"
                      - text: "] to form a lipid peroxyl radical (LOO"
                      - superscript [ref=e559]: •
                      - text: "), which abstracts a H from another unsaturated lipid (LH) molecule to form lipid hydroperoxide (LOOH). B. ROS clearance: There are five major ROS clearance pathways, including I) Superoxide dismutation: Two O"
                      - subscript [ref=e560]: "2"
                      - superscript [ref=e561]: •-
                      - text: can be converted to one H
                      - subscript [ref=e562]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e563]: "2"
                      - text: and O
                      - subscript [ref=e564]: "2"
                      - text: "by SOD1-3. Ⅱ) Hydrogen peroxide decomposition: Two H"
                      - subscript [ref=e565]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e566]: "2"
                      - text: molecules can be decomposed by catalase (CAT) into H
                      - subscript [ref=e567]: "2"
                      - text: O and O
                      - subscript [ref=e568]: "2"
                      - text: ". Ⅲ) Glutathione redox cycle: H"
                      - subscript [ref=e569]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e570]: "2"
                      - text: can be reduced by glutathione peroxidase (GPX) or Peroxiredoxin (PRDX)1/4/6 isoforms into H
                      - subscript [ref=e571]: "2"
                      - text: "O using GSH as the electron donor. Oxidized GSSG will be reduced back to GSH by glutathione reductase (GSR) after receiving a H from NADPH. GPX can also reduce LOOH to become LOH. Ⅳ) Thioredoxin redox cycle: H"
                      - subscript [ref=e572]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e573]: "2"
                      - text: can also be reduced by PRDX1-5 into H
                      - subscript [ref=e574]: "2"
                      - text: O using reduced Thioredoxin (Trx
                      - superscript [ref=e575]: R
                      - text: ) as an electron donor. Oxidized Thioredoxin (Trx
                      - superscript [ref=e576]: O
                      - text: ) will be reduced back to Trx
                      - superscript [ref=e577]: R
                      - text: "by Thioredoxin reductase (TR). Ⅴ) glutathione-S-transferase detoxification: Some electrophilic compounds, such as xenobiotics, can be conjugated with GSH by glutathione-S-transferase (GST) forming Glutathione conjugates (GSX). Abbreviation: Arg, l-arginine; CAT, Catalase; DUOX1/2, Dual oxidase1/2; DHFR, Dihydrofolate reductase; eNOS, Endothelial nitric oxide synthase; ETC, Electron transport chain; GPX, glutathione peroxidase; GSH, Glutathione; GSSG, Glutathione disulfide; GSR, glutathione reductase; G6PD, Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase; GST, glutathione-S-transferase; GSX, Glutathione conjugates; H"
                      - superscript [ref=e578]: •
                      - text: ", Hydrogen atom; H"
                      - superscript [ref=e579]: +
                      - text: ", Hydrogen cation; HOCl, Hypochlorous acid; LH, Unsaturated lipid; L"
                      - superscript [ref=e580]: •
                      - text: ", Lipid radical; LOO"
                      - superscript [ref=e581]: •
                      - text: ", Lipid peroxyl radical; LOOH, Lipid peroxide; MPO, Myeloperoxidase; Mt, Mitochondria; NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e582]: +
                      - text: ", Nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate; NOS, Nitric oxide synthase; NOX1-5, NADPH oxidase complex1-5; ONOO-, Peroxynitrite; PRDX, Peroxiredoxin; RNS, Reactive nitrogen species; ROS, Reactive oxygen species; SOD, superoxide dismutase; TR, Thioredoxin reductase; Trx"
                      - superscript [ref=e583]: R
                      - text: ", Reduced Thioredoxin; Trx"

                  - paragraph [ref=e585]:
                    - strong [ref=e586]: ONOOH
                    - text: — ONOOH is the protonated form of ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e587]: −
                    - text: in solution. In the literature, peroxynitrite is referred to both ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e588]: −
                    - text: and ONOOH. Compared to ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e589]: −
                    - text: ", ONOOH was reported to have a much higher reactivity closed to OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e590]: •
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e591]:
                    - strong [ref=e592]: HOCl
                    - text: — Hypochlorous acid (HOCl) is moderately reactive. It can react with amino and thiol groups, DNA/RNA, fatty acid groups and cholesterol [
                    - link "54" [ref=e593] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib54"
                    - text: "]. Thiols and amino groups are the major targets of HOCl. Oxidation of thiol leads to the formation of disulfide bonds that can result in crosslinking and aggregation of proteins. Oxidation of amino groups yields organic chloramines, which retain some oxidative capacity, leading to increased oxidative damage. HOCl also reacts with unsaturated bonds of fatty acid and cholesterol, generating chlorohydrin that disrupts and increases the permeability of lipid bilayers. Its reaction rate with DNA/RNA is relatively slow. It has been reported that DNA exposed to HOCl leads to decreased viscosity of DNA similarly caused by heat denaturation. HOCl can be directly detected by a recently developed HKOCl-1 probe ["
                    - link "55" [ref=e594] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib55"
                    - text: "]."
                  - paragraph [ref=e595]:
                    - superscript [ref=e596]:
                      - strong [ref=e597]: "1"
                    - strong [ref=e598]: O
                    - subscript [ref=e599]:
                      - strong [ref=e600]: "2"
                    - text: — Singlet oxygen ([
                    - link "1" [ref=e601] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib1"
                    - text: "]Δ"
                    - subscript [ref=e602]: g
                    - text: ) is a strong biological oxidant generated via light-dependent and independent processes [
                    - link "56" [ref=e603] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib56"
                    - text: "]. The light-independent"
                    - superscript [ref=e604]: "1"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e605]: "2"
                    - text: "-produced reaction refers to the chemiexcitation catalyzed by enzymes such as peroxidases and oxygenases. Light-dependent"
                    - superscript [ref=e606]: "1"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e607]: "2"
                    - text: generation occurs when theendogenous sensitisers interact with the UVA component of solar radiation in the presence of molecular oxygen. Main oxidation reactions initiated by
                    - superscript [ref=e608]: "1"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e609]: "2"
                    - text: can result in modifications within key cellular targets including guanine for nucleic acids, unsaturated lipids, and targeted amino acids, which would be implicated in various diseases [
                    - link "56" [ref=e610] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib56"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "57" [ref=e611] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib57"
                    - text: "]. Most recently,"
                    - superscript [ref=e612]: "1"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e613]: "2"
                    - text: also can regulate vascular tone via the tryptophan-derived hydroperoxide as a signaling molecule inducing arterial relaxation [
                    - link "58" [ref=e614] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib58"
                    - text: "]."
              - generic [ref=e615]:
                - heading "3. Reactive oxygen species metabolism (Fig. 1)" [level=2] [ref=e616]:

                  - text: )
                - paragraph [ref=e618]:
                  - strong [ref=e619]: ROS Generation
                  - text: ": ROS is generated in a sequence of reactions where one type of ROS product is used for generating a new type of ROS. ROS generating cascade consists of 5 major pathways, including the generation of O"
                  - subscript [ref=e620]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e621]: •-
                  - text: ", H"
                  - subscript [ref=e622]: "2"
                  - text: O

                  - text: and L

                  - superscript [ref=e626]: •
                  - text: .

                  - strong [ref=e628]: O

                  - strong [ref=e633]: Generation
                  - text: — O
                  - subscript [ref=e634]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e635]: •-
                  - text: is usually the first ROS generating cascade. It is generated from the coupling of O
                  - subscript [ref=e636]: "2"
                  - text: with an electron (
                  - emphasis [ref=e637]: e
                  - superscript [ref=e638]: −
                  - text: ) from e
                  - superscript [ref=e639]: −
                  - text: donor, which usually are reduced nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NADH) or reduced nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADPH) in mammalian cells. This one
                  - emphasis [ref=e640]: e
                  - superscript [ref=e641]:
                    - emphasis [ref=e642]: "-"
                  - text: reduction of O
                  - subscript [ref=e643]: "2"
                  - text: produces a charged ionic species with a single unpaired electron and a net negative charge of −1 (
                  - superscript [ref=e644]: .
                  - text: Ö̤:Ö̤
                  - superscript [ref=e645]: .
                  - text: + e
                  - superscript [ref=e646]: −
                  - text: →:Ö̤:Ö̤
                  - superscript [ref=e647]: .-
                  - text: ). The coupling of the
                  - emphasis [ref=e648]: e
                  - superscript [ref=e649]:
                    - emphasis [ref=e650]: "-"
                  - text: with O
                  - subscript [ref=e651]: "2"
                  - text: can be catalyzed by various oxidases including NADPH oxidase (NOX1/2/3/5 family), uncoupled NOS, xanthine oxidase (XO) and complex I/II/III/IV of mitochondria (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e652] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig1"
                  - text: ","

                  - text: .
                - paragraph [ref=e654]:
                  - strong [ref=e655]: RNS generation
                  - text: — RNS are the derivatives of NO
                  - superscript [ref=e656]: •
                  - text: which is generated from l-arginine (L-Arg) and catalyzed by NOS. NO
                  - superscript [ref=e657]: •
                  - text: can rapidly react with O
                  - subscript [ref=e658]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e659]: •-
                  - text: to form ONOO
                  - superscript [ref=e660]: −
                  - text: . The second order rate constant between NO
                  - superscript [ref=e661]: •
                  - text: with O
                  - subscript [ref=e662]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e663]: •-
                  - text: is nearly 10 times faster than SOD-catalyzed dismutation of O
                  - subscript [ref=e664]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e665]: •-
                  - text: (1.9 × 10
                  - superscript [ref=e666]: "10"
                  - text: M
                  - superscript [ref=e667]: −1
                  - text: s

                  - text: M

                  - text: s
                  - superscript [ref=e671]: −1
                  - text: ) [

                    - /url: "#bib23"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "59" [ref=e673] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib59"
                  - text: "]. However, due to the high containment of cellular SOD (1000 times more than intracellular NO"
                  - superscript [ref=e674]: •
                  - text: level) at physiological conditions, O
                  - subscript [ref=e675]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e676]: •-
                  - text: is eliminated before encountering NO
                  - superscript [ref=e677]: •
                  - text: . In addition, the alternative pathway of exogenous NO
                  - subscript [ref=e678]: "3"
                  - superscript [ref=e679]: "-"
                  - text: "-NO"
                  - subscript [ref=e680]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e681]: "-"
                  - text: "-NO is enhanced when the production of endogenous NO from l-arginine is inhibited due to hypoxia or ischemia ["
                  - link "60" [ref=e682] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib60"
                  - text: "]. ONOO"
                  - superscript [ref=e683]: −
                  - text: can be produced via the reaction between NO and O
                  - subscript [ref=e684]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e685]: •-
                  - text: only in pathological conditions [
                  - link "52" [ref=e686] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib52"
                  - text: "]. In a biological system, ONOO"
                  - superscript [ref=e687]: −
                  - text: is in equilibrium with its neutral form peroxynitrous acid (ONOOH) which has a pKa = 6.8 [
                  - link "61" [ref=e688] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib61"
                  - text: "]. At physiological pH (7.4), the estimated proportion of ONOO"
                  - superscript [ref=e689]: −
                  - text: and ONOOH is ∼80% and ∼20%, respectively. In aqueous phase, ONOO
                  - superscript [ref=e690]: −
                  - text: predominantly reacts with carbon dioxide (CO
                  - subscript [ref=e691]: "2"
                  - text: ) to form a transient adduct nitrosoperoxycarbonate (ONOOCO
                  - subscript [ref=e692]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e693]: "-"
                  - text: ), 2/3 of which is decomposed into CO
                  - subscript [ref=e694]: "2"
                  - text: and a relatively inert nitrate anion (NO
                  - subscript [ref=e695]: "3"
                  - superscript [ref=e696]: "-"
                  - text: ), and 1/3 of which is decomposed to toxic CO
                  - subscript [ref=e697]: "3"
                  - superscript [ref=e698]: •-
                  - text: and NO
                  - subscript [ref=e699]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e700]: •
                  - text: . ONOOH can undergo homolysis fission to generate the OH
                  - superscript [ref=e701]: •
                  - text: and NO
                  - subscript [ref=e702]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e703]: •
                  - text: . However, the reaction rate is very slow in the aqueous phase and can greatly speed up when ONOOH goes into lipid membrane
                  - strong [ref=e704]: (

                  - text: .

                  - text: — OH

                  - text: via a metal ion (iron or copper) catalyzed by Habor-Weiss reaction
                  - strong [ref=e760]: (

                    - /url: "#fig1"

                  - text: . Harbor-Weiss is divided into two steps. The first step involves the reduction of Fe

                  - text: by O

                  - text: O

                  - text: to generate OH

                  - text: and OH
                  - superscript [ref=e771]: −
                  - text: (Fenton reaction). In diseases accompanied by iron accumulation, which is observed in atherosclerotic lesion [

                    - /url: "#bib62"
                  - text: "] or in sickle cell patients ["

                  - text: "], OH"
                  - superscript [ref=e774]: •
                  - text: mediated oxidative stress may be a major underlying mechanism. OH

                  - text: can also be generated from the MPO-catalyzed reaction between HOCl and O

                  - text: can react rapidly with O

                  - text: to generate lipid peroxyl radical (LOO
                  - superscript [ref=e793]: •
                  - text: ), which is a moderate oxidant capable of abstracting the H from nearby lipid to generate lipid hydroperoxide (LOOH) and a new L
                  - superscript [ref=e794]: •
                  - text: . The reaction propagates until the lipid radicals react with scavenging antioxidants or other free-radicals to form a covalent bond
                  - strong [ref=e795]: (
