                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1069] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/tbl2/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1072]:
                    - text: "Antioxidants consist of two classes: enzymatic and non-enzymatic. This table describes the antioxidant distribution, target and function."
                    - strong [ref=e1073]: "Abbreviation:"
                    - text: Akt, Protein kinase B; CAT, catalase; Cys, Cysteine; Cyto, Cytosol; ExC, Extracellular space; ER, Endoplasmic reticulum; GF, Growth factor; GI, Gastrointestinal; GPX1-8, glutathione peroxidase1-8; GSH, Glutathione; GSR, glutathione reductase; GSSG, Glutathione disulfide; GST, Glutathione-S-transferase; H
                    - subscript [ref=e1074]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1075]: "2"
                    - text: ", Hydrogen peroxide; IM, Intermembrane space; LOO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1076]: •
                    - text: ", Lipid peroxyl radical; Mt, Mitochondria; NOX1, NADPH oxidase complex1; Nu, Nucleus; O2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1077]: •-
                    - text: ", Superoxide radical anion; OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e1078]: •
                    - text: ", Hydroxyl radical; ONOO-, Peroxynitrite; PM, Plasma Membrane; PRDX1∼6, Peroxiredoxin1∼6; PCOOH, phosphatidylcholine hydroperoxide; ROO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1079]: •
                    - text: ", Peroxyl radical; ROOH, Organic peroxide; ROS, Reactive oxygen species; SMC Smooth muscular cell; SOD1∼3, Superoxide dismutase1∼3; TNFα, Tumor necrosis factor alpha; TRX, Thioredoxin; TO•, Vitamin E radical."
                - paragraph [ref=e1080]: "ROS metabolic clearance is involved with antioxidants. The aerobic organisms evolved an efficient antioxidant system which can be classified into two categories: enzymatic antioxidants and non-enzymatic antioxidants."
                - generic [ref=e1081]:
                  - heading "4.1. Enzymatic antioxidants" [level=3] [ref=e1082]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1083]:
                    - strong [ref=e1084]: SOD
                    - text: — SOD is responsible for the dismutation of O
                    - subscript [ref=e1085]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1086]: •-
                    - text: (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1087] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ","
                    - strong [ref=e1088]: clearance pathway I
                    - text: "). In human, there are three isoforms of SOD: SOD1 (Cu/Zn), SOD2 (Mn) and SOD3 (Cu/Zn) ["
                    - link "66" [ref=e1089] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib66"
                    - text: "]. All three isoforms rely on their containing metals for their dismutation activity. SOD1 is widely distributed in all tissues and cells. It is the major isoform for clearance of O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1090]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1091]: •-
                    - text: generated in the cytosol and intermembrane space of mitochondria. Within the mitochondria, it interacts with BCL2 and prevents apoptosis of spinal cord neurons. SOD1 mutation is implicated in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis [
                    - link "67" [ref=e1092] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib67"
                    - text: "]. SOD2 is also widely distributed in most tissues and cells. It is the major isoform for the clearance of O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1093]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1094]: •-
                    - text: within the mitochondrial matrix, conferring strong protection against apoptosis of cells, in particular neurons, myocytes, and cardiomyocyte (CM) [
                    - link "66" [ref=e1095] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib66"
                    - text: "]. SOD2 deficiency is highly linked with neurodegenerative disease, skeletal muscle disease, and cardiac dysfunction. Unlike SOD1 and SOD2 which are ubiquitous, SOD3 has the highest expression in vessel, lung, and kidney. Moreover, SOD3 is secreted to extracellular spaces (ExC), anchored to the extracellular matrix and is the only isoform protecting against the extracellular O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1096]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1097]: •-
                    - text: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e1098]:
                    - strong [ref=e1099]: CAT
                    - text: — CAT is an iron-containing peroxidase to catalyze two H
                    - subscript [ref=e1100]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1101]: "2"
                    - text: into two H
                    - subscript [ref=e1102]: "2"
                    - text: O and one O
                    - subscript [ref=e1103]: "2"
                    - text: (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1104] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ","
                    - strong [ref=e1105]: clearance pathway II
                    - text: ). The second order rate constant of reaction with peroxide is 8 × 10
                    - superscript [ref=e1106]: "6"
                    - text: M
                    - superscript [ref=e1107]: −1
                    - text: s
                    - superscript [ref=e1108]: −1
                    - text: "["
                    - link "68" [ref=e1109] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib68"
                    - text: "]. In mammals, it is predominately expressed in liver and erythrocytes ["
                    - link "69" [ref=e1110] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib69"
                    - text: "], and located primarily in peroxisomes where H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1111]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1112]: "2"
                    - text: is formed during the breakdown of various substrates by flavoprotein oxidases. The role of CAT in cytosolic or nucleic H
                    - subscript [ref=e1113]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1114]: "2"
                    - text: control is not so important. It does this job only when the H
                    - subscript [ref=e1115]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1116]: "2"
                    - text: level is far above the physiological levels such as in oxidative stress. CAT is mainly designed for ethanol metabolism to turn alcohol to acetaldehyde. CAT is far less expressed than GPX and PRDX, which are mainly designed for H
                    - subscript [ref=e1117]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1118]: "2"
                    - text: decomposition. Unlike GPX or PRDX, CAT does not require any reducing agent to recover its reductive state and the activity.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1119]:
                    - strong [ref=e1120]: GPX
                    - text: — GPX is a family of selenium-containing peroxidases (except GPX5) transforming the H
                    - subscript [ref=e1121]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1122]: "2"
                    - text: to H
                    - subscript [ref=e1123]: "2"
                    - text: O, or converting the organic hydroperoxide (ROOH) to corresponding alcohol (ROH). The second order rate constant of reaction with hydrogen peroxide is ∼4.0 × 10
                    - superscript [ref=e1124]: "7"
                    - text: M
                    - superscript [ref=e1125]: −1
                    - text: s
                    - superscript [ref=e1126]: −1
                    - text: "["
                    - link "70" [ref=e1127] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib70"
                    - text: "]. In the glutathione redox cycle, GPX reduces H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1128]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1129]: "2"
                    - text: or ROOH and become oxidized (GPX
                    - superscript [ref=e1130]: O
                    - text: ) and inactive. GPX
                    - superscript [ref=e1131]: O
                    - text: can be converted back to its reduced form (GPX
                    - superscript [ref=e1132]: R
                    - text: ) by GSH. GSH becomes glutathione disulfide (GSSG), which is reduced back to GSH after a hydride (H-) is transferred from NADPH catalyzed by glutathione reductase (GSR
                    - strong [ref=e1133]: ) (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1134] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ","
                    - strong [ref=e1135]: clearance pathway III).
                    - text: Unlike CAT, GPX senses small elevation of H
                    - subscript [ref=e1136]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1137]: "2"
                    - text: . In human, eight isoforms of GPX (GPX1-8) have been identified, which vary by distribution, ROS targets and biological functions [
                    - link "71" [ref=e1138] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib71"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "72" [ref=e1139] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib72"
                    - text: "] ("
                    - link "Table 2" [ref=e1140] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tbl2"
                    - text: ). GPX1 is the most abundant isoform and ubiquitously expressed in most tissue, mostly found in the cytosol and mitochondrial matrix, and mainly used for H
                    - subscript [ref=e1141]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1142]: "2"
                    - text: clearance. GPX2 is restricted to gastrointestinal epithelium, detected in EC, mainly located at ExC and mainly targeting H
                    - subscript [ref=e1143]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1144]: "2."
                    - text: GPX3 is primarily found in plasma, highly expressed in kidney and lung, mainly located in the cytosol, and targeting on H
                    - subscript [ref=e1145]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1146]: "2"
                    - text: . GPX4 is a phospholipid hydroperoxidase. It is ubiquitously expressed, found in the cytosol, mitochondria, and nucleus, and targets peroxidized lipid (ROOH). GPX5 is specifically expressed in the epididymis, critical for protecting spermatozoa against lipid peroxidation. GPX6 is restricted to in embryo and olfactory epithelium and mainly targeting on H
                    - subscript [ref=e1147]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1148]: "2"
                    - text: . GPX7 is found in Leydig cell, hepatocyte, and adrenal gland. GPX7 is involved in detoxification of H
                    - subscript [ref=e1149]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1150]: "2"
                    - text: in esophageal epithelia. GPX8 is found in ExC and endoplasmic reticulum (ER), targeting on H
                    - subscript [ref=e1151]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1152]: "2"
                    - text: and involved in Linoleic acid metabolism.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1153]:
                    - strong [ref=e1154]: PRDX
                    - text: — PRDX is a family of thiol peroxidases also catalyzing the reduction of H
                    - subscript [ref=e1155]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1156]: 2 to
                    - text: H
                    - subscript [ref=e1157]: "2"
                    - text: O, or ROOH to ROH. Their second order rate constant of reaction with peroxide is ∼10
                    - superscript [ref=e1158]: 6−7
                    - text: M
                    - superscript [ref=e1159]: −1
                    - text: s
                    - superscript [ref=e1160]: −1
                    - text: "["
                    - link "73" [ref=e1161] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib73"
                    - text: "]. In the thioredoxin redox cycle, PRDX reduces H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1162]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1163]: "2"
                    - text: or ROH and become oxidized (PRDX
                    - superscript [ref=e1164]: O
                    - text: ) and inactive. PRDX
                    - superscript [ref=e1165]: O
                    - text: can be converted back to its reduced form (PRDX
                    - superscript [ref=e1166]: R
                    - text: ) by TRX
                    - superscript [ref=e1167]: R
                    - text: . TRX
                    - superscript [ref=e1168]: R
                    - text: is oxidized to TRX
                    - superscript [ref=e1169]: O
                    - strong [ref=e1170]: (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1171] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ","
                    - strong [ref=e1172]: clearance pathway Ⅳ).
                    - text: In mammals, six isoforms (PRDX1-6) have been identified [
                    - link "74" [ref=e1173] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib74"
                    - text: "]. All isoforms contain a conserved cysteine residue (C"
                    - superscript [ref=e1174]: P
                    - text: ), which can be oxidized by H
                    - subscript [ref=e1175]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1176]: "2"
                    - text: or ROOH into sulfenic acid (C
                    - superscript [ref=e1177]: P
                    - text: –SOH). C
                    - superscript [ref=e1178]: P
                    - text: "-SOH then reacts with another semi-conserved Cys residue (C"
                    - superscript [ref=e1179]: R
                    - text: ) to form a disulfide bond. Based on the location or absence of the C
                    - superscript [ref=e1180]: R
                    - text: ", PRDX is classified as typical 2-Cys PRDX (PRDX1-4), atypical 2-Cys PRDXs (PRDX5) and 1-Cys PRDXs (PRDX6). Both typical 2-cys PRDXs and atypical 2-cys PRDXs are heterodimers containing both C"
                    - superscript [ref=e1181]: P
                    - text: and C
                    - superscript [ref=e1182]: R
                    - text: in each subunit. For typical 2-cys PRDXs, C
                    - superscript [ref=e1183]: P
                    - text: "-SOH reacts with the CR from another subunit to form an intersubunit disulfide bond. For atypical 2-Cys PRDXs, C"
                    - superscript [ref=e1184]: P
                    - text: –SOH reacts with C
                    - superscript [ref=e1185]: R
                    - text: from the same subunit. For the 1-Cys PPRDXs, C
                    - superscript [ref=e1186]: P
                    - text: "-SOH reacts with C"
                    - superscript [ref=e1187]: R
                    - text: from other molecules. PRDX isoforms vary in their distribution and biological function. In general, PRDX1 is ubiquitously expressed, found in the cytosol, and involved in cell cycle control and Akt-driven tumorigenesis [
                    - link "75" [ref=e1188] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib75"
                    - text: "]. PRDX2 is also ubiquitously expressed but with especially high expression in erythrocyte, found in the cytosol and involved in regulating the antiviral activity of CD8"
                    - superscript [ref=e1189]: +
                    - text: T cells, growth factor, and TNFα signaling [
                    - link "76" [ref=e1190] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib76"
                    - text: "]. PRDX3 is mostly expressed in ovary, located in mitochondria and involved in cell proliferation and differentiation regulation. PRDX4 exists predominately in plasma, anchored to the extracellular space (ExC) of EC and protecting the EC from oxidative damage. PRDX5 is highly expressed in tendon fibroblast, found at mitochondria, cytosol, and nucleus, and is involved in inhibiting p53-induced ROS generation, preventing apoptosis of tendon fibroblast and tendon degeneration ["
                    - link "77" [ref=e1191] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib77"
                    - text: "]. PRDX5 is the most efficient antioxidant for ONOO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1192]: −
                    - text: removal. PRDX6 can be found in plasma, thyroid gland, kidney, and trachea (Clara cells), and mainly located in the cytosol. PRDX6 is also equipped with Phospholipase A2 activity, involved in the turnover of Phospholipid and regulation of NOX1 activity [41].
                - generic [ref=e1193]:
                  - heading "4.2. Non-enzymatic antioxidants" [level=3] [ref=e1194]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1195]:
                    - strong [ref=e1196]: GSH
                    - text: "— GSH is the most abundant (5–10 mM) intracellular antioxidant ubiquitously expressed, with the highest concentration in the liver. It is the major antioxidant maintaining the whole body's redox status and often referred to as the body's master antioxidant. It is a tripeptide composed of three amino acids: Cys, glycine, and glutamate. The sulfhydryl (SH) group on Cys residue accounts for its strong e−donating feature. As described above in"
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e1197] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#fig1"
                    - text: ", GSH can be used as the reductant of GPX"
                    - superscript [ref=e1198]: O
                    - text: (
                    - strong [ref=e1199]: clearance pathway III
                    - text: ) or used by glutathione S-transferases (GST) for detoxification of xenobiotics (
                    - strong [ref=e1200]: clearance pathway Ⅴ
                    - text: ). In addition, GSH serves as a reductant of a variety of oxidized antioxidants—such as vitamin C and Vitamin E.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1201]:
                    - strong [ref=e1202]: TRX
                    - text: "— TRX is also ubiquitously expressed. In the mammal cell, there are two types of TRX: TRX1 is a cytosolic and extracellular enzyme while TRX2 is the mitochondrial specific enzyme. Both TRX1/2 serve as e"
                    - superscript [ref=e1203]: −
                    - text: donor for reducing the oxidized PRDX1-5
                    - strong [ref=e1204]: .
                  - paragraph [ref=e1205]:
                    - strong [ref=e1206]: Uric acid
                    - text: — Uric acid is the final oxidation product of purine metabolism. In healthy human plasma, the uric acid level is ∼300 μM. In plasma, uric acid functions as antioxidant by its direct scavenging of ROS or by modifying the activity of xanthine oxidase (XO). It was claimed that more than half of antioxidant capacity comes from uric acid in human blood. The scavenging ability of uric acid highly depends on the environment. In the aqueous phase, uric acid is a powerful scavenger for R
                    - superscript [ref=e1207]: •
                    - text: ", ROO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1208]: •
                    - text: ", OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e1209]: •
                    - text: ", OHCL and ONOO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1210]: −
                    - text: ", but not for O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1211]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1212]: •-
                    - text: . In the hydrophobic phase, uric acid greatly loses antioxidant ability.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1213]:
                    - strong [ref=e1214]: Vitamin E
                    - text: — Vitamin E is lipid soluble and the major antioxidant found in the lipid phase of membrane. It reacts with lipid radicals leading to the termination of membrane lipid peroxidation.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1215]:
                    - strong [ref=e1216]: Vitamin C
                    - text: — Vitamin C is water-soluble and capable of reacting with a wide variety of ROS, including OH
                    - superscript [ref=e1217]: •
                    - text: ", R"
                    - superscript [ref=e1218]: •
                    - text: ", ROO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1219]: •
                    - text: ", TO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1220]: •
                    - text: ", O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1221]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1222]: •-
                    - text: and ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e1223]: −
                    - text: . Ascorbate can donate electrons to highly reactive free radicals such as hydrogen peroxide and is converted to the ascorbyl radicals via monodehydroascorbate reductase [
                    - link "78" [ref=e1224] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib78"
                    - text: "]. Monodehydroascorbate radical is rapidly dismutated into ascorbate and dehydroascorbate with catalyzation of NADH-dependent semidehydroascorbate reductase. Dehydroascorbate can be converted to ascorbate by dehydroascorbate reductase ["
                    - link "79" [ref=e1225] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib79"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "80" [ref=e1226] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib80"
                    - text: "]. Vitamin C helps the reduction of oxidized Vitamin E during lipid peroxidation. Ascorbate was shown to stabilize the eNOS cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin for increasing NOS enzyme activity in vitro and vivo ["
                    - link "81" [ref=e1227] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib81"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "82" [ref=e1228] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib82"
                    - text: "]. However, it also steadily increased eNOS activity via increased phosphorylation at eNOS-Ser"
                    - superscript [ref=e1229]: "1177"
                    - text: and concomitantly decreased phosphorylation at eNOS-Thr
                    - superscript [ref=e1230]: "495"
                    - text: ", which is independent of its effects on BH4 stabilization ["
                    - link "83" [ref=e1231] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib83"
                    - text: "]. In some situation, Vitamin C can be a prooxidant, as it can reduce the high valent transition metal to low valent form which will drive the Fenton reaction and increase the generation of toxic OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e1232]: •
                    - text: radical.
                  - paragraph [ref=e1233]:
                    - strong [ref=e1234]: Bilirubin
                    - text: — Bilirubin is the end product of heme metabolism by two sequential reactions catalyzed by heme oxygenase and biliverdin reductase [
                    - link "84" [ref=e1235] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib84"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "85" [ref=e1236] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib85"
                    - text: "]. The lipophilic bilirubin can act as antioxidants to protect lipids from oxidation ["
                    - link "86" [ref=e1237] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib86"
                    - text: "]. Mice with deletion of heme oxygenase-2 or biliverdin reductase display greater lipid than protein oxidation. Conversion of biliverdin to bilirubin by biliverdin reductase contributes to endothelial cell protection against ROS because bilirubin but not biliverdin increased expression of the protective protein GTP cyclohydrolase in ECs ["
                    - link "87" [ref=e1238] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bib87"
                    - text: "]."

                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1335] [cursor=pointer]:

                  - paragraph [ref=e1338]:
                    - text: Major intracellular ROS generators are described for their distributions, products, and relevant functions.
                    - strong [ref=e1339]: Abbreviation
                    - text: ": ATP, Adenosine Triphosphate; CM, Cardiomyocytes; Cyto, Cytosol; DUOX1/2, Dual oxidase 1/2; EC, Endothelial cells; ER, Endoplasmic reticulum; ES, Endosome; EXC, Extracellular space; eNOS, Endothelial nitric oxide synthase; ETC, Electron transport chain; FA, Focal adhesion; HOCl, Hypochlorous acid; H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1340]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1341]: "2"
                    - text: ", hydrogen peroxide; Mt, mitochondria; MPO, Myeloperoxidase; MC, Monocyte; NOX1∼5, NADPH oxidase complex 1–5; NO, Nitric oxide; O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1342]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1343]: •-
                    - text: ", Superoxide radical anion; PM, Plasma membrane; ROS, Reactive oxygen species; VSMC, Vascular smooth muscle cells; XOR, xanthine oxidoreductase."
                - paragraph [ref=e1344]:
                  - strong [ref=e1345]: Mitochondrial ETC
                  - text: — ROS detected in mitochondria can be generated by the Mitochondrial-localized oxidases [
                  - link "22" [ref=e1346] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib22"
                  - text: "]. The vast majority of Mitochondrial ROS is generated from the mitochondrial electric transport chain (ETC), especially from complex I (Com I) and Com III ("
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1347] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig2"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1348]: pathway 1a
                  - text: ). Their relative contribution to overall mitochondrial O
                  - subscript [ref=e1349]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1350]: •-
                  - text: production is tissue specific, dependent on pathophysiological conditions, respiring state and redox potential of Mitochondrial ETC. In the normal condition, Com I was reported as the primary source of Mitochondrial ROS in brain, while Com III contributed to the major Mitochondrial ROS in the heart and lung [
                  - link "88" [ref=e1351] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib88"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "89" [ref=e1352] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib89"
                  - text: "]. In pathological conditions like ischemia and cell apoptosis, Com IV can be inhibited and then O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1353]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1354]: •-
                  - text: was mainly released from Com III. The importance of Mitochondrial ROS in CVD pathogenesis is emphasized by the observation that animals with deficiency of the Mitochondrial specific O
                  - subscript [ref=e1355]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1356]: •-
                  - text: scavenger SOD2 can result in severe cardiac dysfunction [
                  - link "90" [ref=e1357] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib90"
                  - text: "]. SOD2 deficiency increased mitochondrial oxidative stress and aggravate age-dependent vascular endothelial dysfunction due to ROS-mediated mitochondrial DNA damage ["
                  - link "91" [ref=e1358] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib91"
                  - text: "]. Overexpression of a mitochondrial specific H"
                  - subscript [ref=e1359]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1360]: "2"
                  - text: scavenger peroxiredoxin-3 (PRDX3) can prevent myocardial infarction (MI)-induced heart failure in mice [
                  - link "92" [ref=e1361] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib92"
                  - text: "]. Besides, Mitochondrial ROS induced by various CVD risk factors are involved in EC activation/dysfunction, SMC proliferation/migration, dendritic cell differentiation, atherosclerosis, and other inflammatory disease ["
                  - link "93" [ref=e1362] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib93"
                  - text: "]."
                - figure [ref=e1363]:
                  - heading "Fig. 2." [level=3] [ref=e1364]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1365]:
                    - link "Fig. 2 🔍" [ref=e1366] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=6831867_gr2.jpg
                      - img "Fig. 2" [ref=e1367]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1369] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/fig2/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1371]:
                    - text: "Metabolic interplay between various ROS generators. ROS generator interplay is summarized in three major pathways: 1) NOX-ROS promoted Mt-ROS generation. In response to activation signaling, such as AngII, NOX1/2 located in PM/ER/ES can be activated via PKC-induced P47phox phosphorylation. Extracellular O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1372]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1373]: •-
                    - text: generated from PM-located NOX1/2 can be dismutated by SOD3 to form H
                    - subscript [ref=e1374]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1375]: "2"
                    - text: which then diffuses into the cytosol via AQP, while intracellular O
                    - subscript [ref=e1376]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1377]: •-
                    - text: generated from ER/ES-located NOX1/2 can be dismutated by SOD1 to form H
                    - subscript [ref=e1378]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1379]: "2"
                    - text: . 1a) ROS produced by Mt ETC. NOX1/2-derived H
                    - subscript [ref=e1380]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1381]: "2"
                    - text: can activate Mt-KATP via PKC-ε signaling, leading to the K
                    - superscript [ref=e1382]: +
                    - text: influx, decrease of Mt membrane potential (ΔΨm), inhibition of Mt complex I (Com I) and finally the increase of Mt O
                    - subscript [ref=e1383]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1384]: •-
                    - text: production. 1b) ROS produced by NOX4 in Mt. In certain cell types such as CM and EC, NOX4 isoform is detected at the Mt. Increase expression of NOX4 by activation signaling such as AngII/Gαq pathway can directly contribute to the increase of Mt ROS. 2) Mt-ROS activated NOX-ROS generation. In Mt, O
                    - subscript [ref=e1385]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1386]: •-
                    - text: generated by Com I is mainly released to Mt matrix and dismutated by SOD2 to H
                    - subscript [ref=e1387]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1388]: "2"
                    - text: ", while O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1389]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1390]: •-
                    - text: generated by Com III can be released to either Mt matrix or intermembrane space and dismutated by SOD1. A small amount of Mt O
                    - subscript [ref=e1391]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1392]: •-
                    - text: can be transported to the cytosol by VDAC directly and then dismutated by SOD1 to form H
                    - subscript [ref=e1393]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1394]: "2"
                    - text: . Mt-generated H
                    - subscript [ref=e1395]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1396]: "2"
                    - text: can be leaked to the cytosol through water channel aquaporin (AQP). Mt-derived H
                    - subscript [ref=e1397]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1398]: "2"
                    - text: "then further activates NADPH oxidase by two pathways: 2a) The p47phox phosphorylation via activating PKC/cSrc; 2b) The increased expression of P22phox/NOX1/2/4 probably via c-Src activation. 3) NOX/Mt-ROS regulated eNOS/XR-ROS. Cytosolic O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1399]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1400]: •-
                    - text: from Mt or NADPH oxidase can react with NO to form ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e1401]: −
                    - text: ", resulting in eNOS uncoupling via depletion of BH4, or XR to XO transformation via oxidation of critical thiols in Cys535/992. O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1402]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1403]: •-
                    - text: "is generated by XO and uncoupled eNOS. Abbreviation: AngII, Angiotensin II; AT1R, Angiotensin II receptor type 1; AQP, Aquaporin; BH2, dihydrobiopterin; BH4, Tetrahydrobiopterin; eNOS, Endothelial nitric oxide synthase; ER, Endoplasmic reticulum; ES, Endosome; ETC, Electron transportation chain; H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1404]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1405]: "2"
                    - text: ", hydrogen peroxide; Mt-KATP, Mitochondrial adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-sensitive potassium channel; NO, nitric oxide; NOX, NADPH oxidase; ONOO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1406]: −
                    - text: ", Peroxynitrite; p-P47phox, phosphorylated P47phox; PKC-ε, PKC-epsilon; PM, plasma membrane; SOD, Superoxide dismutase; U-eNOS, uncoupled eNOS; VSMC, Vascular smooth muscle cells; VDAC, Voltage-dependent anion channel; XO, Xanthine oxidase; XR, Xanthine reductase; ΔΨm, Mitochondria membrane potential."
                - paragraph [ref=e1407]:
                  - strong [ref=e1408]: NOS
                  - text: "— NOS belongs to the family of enzymes that catalyzed the production of NO from L-Arg. In mammalian cells, it consists of three isoforms: eNOS (EC, CM), iNOS (VSMC, MC) and nNOS (neuron, skeletal muscle) ["
                  - link "94" [ref=e1409] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib94"
                  - text: "]. Within ECs, eNOS localizes at the periphery of the golgi complex and cholesterol-rich microdomains of the plasma membrane (PM) such as caveolae, as homodimers. Deficiency of cofactor BH4 or substrate L-Arg leads to the electron transferring directly to free O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1410]: "2"
                  - text: for the formation of O
                  - subscript [ref=e1411]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1412]: •-
                  - text: ", a phenomenon called “NOS uncoupling”. Another direct redox-regulatory pathway for eNOS function is the oxidative disruption of the zinc-sulfur complex in the binding region of the eNOS dimer, caused by peroxynitrite and hypochlorous acid, resulting in the loss of functional dimers ["
                  - link "95" [ref=e1413] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib95"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "96" [ref=e1414] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib96"
                  - text: "]. NOS uncoupling can reduce NO generation and increase O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1415]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1416]: •-
                  - text: production. NO is a cardio-protective RNS while O
                  - subscript [ref=e1417]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1418]: •-
                  - text: is toxic to the cell in most case. The uncoupling process transforms eNOS from a cardio-protective enzyme to an oxidative stress contributor in CVD [
                  - link "97" [ref=e1419] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib97"
                  - text: "]. Interestingly, BH4 is highly sensitive to oxidant, particularly ONOO"
                  - superscript [ref=e1420]: −
                  - text: ", and often oxidized to BH2, suggesting that oxidative stress-mediated BH4 depletion contributes greatly to NOS uncoupling ("
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1421] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig2"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1422]: pathway 3
                  - text: ). NOS uncoupling can also be induced by other mechanisms [
                  - link "98" [ref=e1423] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib98"
                  - text: "], such as oxidative stress-mediated S-glutathionylation of NOS. eNOS may interact with mitochondrial outer membrane porin and modulate mitochondrial ROS production. Blocking this interaction decreased NO release and increased mitochondrial ROS levels in the fetal pulmonary artery endothelial cells ["
                  - link "99" [ref=e1424] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib99"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e1425]:
                  - strong [ref=e1426]: XOR
                  - text: — XOR is a purine degrading enzyme converting hypoxanthine to xanthine and finally to uric acid. It is widely distributed, with the highest expression/activity in liver, intestine, and mammary epithelial cells and capillary EC [
                  - link "100" [ref=e1427] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib100"
                  - text: "]. XOR consists of two interconvertible forms, xanthine oxidase (XO) and xanthine oxidoreductase /dehydrogenase (XOR/XDH) ("
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1428] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig2"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1429]: pathway 3
                  - text: ). XOR can be converted to XO either irreversibly by proteolysis or reversibly by oxidation of Cys [
                  - link "101" [ref=e1430] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib101"
                  - text: "]. Like NOS uncoupling, ONOO"
                  - superscript [ref=e1431]: −
                  - text: can promote the Cys oxidation and subsequent XDH/XO transformation. XDH isoform is able to oxidize NADH under acidic conditions (pH ∼6.5) to produce O
                  - subscript [ref=e1432]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1433]: •-
                  - text: in the early stage of ischemia-reperfusion (IR) [
                  - link "102" [ref=e1434] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib102"
                  - text: "]. In the late stage, XO isoform may be the quantitatively important source of O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1435]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1436]: •-
                  - text: . XOR as a source of ROS contributes to oxidative stress in a variety of disease, particularly IR injury [
                  - link "103" [ref=e1437] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib103"
                  - text: "]. Moreover, IR injury induces the production of inflammatory cytokines which can transcriptionally regulate XDH and increase XO activity ["
                  - link "102" [ref=e1438] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib102"
                  - text: "]. XOR-derived ROS also seem to play a more important role in XO enriched tissue such as liver and intestine than some tissue lacking XO activity such as heart ["
                  - link "102" [ref=e1439] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib102"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "104" [ref=e1440] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib104"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "105" [ref=e1441] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib105"
                  - text: "]. However, it is premature to conclude the exact effect of XO-derived ROS in CVD, because most of the findings are based on the use of allopurinol/oxypurinol for the inhibition of XOR."
                - paragraph [ref=e1442]:
                  - strong [ref=e1443]: NOX
                  - text: — unlike other ROS generating enzymes in which ROS are byproducts, NADPH oxidase directly catalyzes O
                  - subscript [ref=e1444]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1445]: •−
                  - text: or H
                  - subscript [ref=e1446]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1447]: "2"
                  - text: production [
                  - link "106" [ref=e1448] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib106"
                  - text: "]. It is a membrane-bound enzyme complex consisting of seven members in mammalian cells, named by their core catalytic subunit: NOX1, NOX2, NOX3, NOX4, NOX5, DUOX1 and DUOX2.["
                  - link "107" [ref=e1449] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib107"
                  - text: "]. NOX2 family is the prototype of NADPH oxidase first described in phagocytes for its critical role in microbial killing via generating oxidative burst ["
                  - link "108" [ref=e1450] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib108"
                  - text: "]. It was found to be widely expressed in the cardiovascular system including EC, VSMC from small arteries, CM, and adventitial fibroblasts ["
                  - link "109" [ref=e1451] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib109"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "110" [ref=e1452] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib110"
                  - text: "]. NOX1 family was first identified in colon epithelial cells ["
                  - link "111" [ref=e1453] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib111"
                  - text: "]. It was also detected in the cardiovascular system including EC, VSMC, and CM. NOX3 family is expressed almost exclusively in the inner ear, playing an important role in the perception of balance and gravity ["
                  - link "112" [ref=e1454] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib112"
                  - text: "]. NOX4 family was originally identified in meningeal cells of kidney ["
                  - link "113" [ref=e1455] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib113"
                  - text: "]. It was also highly expressed and as a major NOX isoform in MC /Mφ and nearly all vascular cells including EC, VSMC, and CM. NOX5 family was first discovered in human spleen, testis, and kidney ["
                  - link "114" [ref=e1456] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib114"
                  - text: "]. It was the only NOX isoform missing in rodents. In the human vasculature, NOX5 was found expressed in EC, VSMC, and CM, but not in adventitial fibroblasts ["
                  - link "115" [ref=e1457] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib115"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "116" [ref=e1458] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib116"
                  - text: "]. NOX5 was also found constitutively expressed in MC/Mφ ["
                  - link "117" [ref=e1459] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib117"
                  - text: "]. DUOX1/2 family is discovered in thyroid epithelial cells and has its function as oxidase to generate ROS ["
                  - link "118" [ref=e1460] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib118"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "119" [ref=e1461] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib119"
                  - text: "]. But there is a paucity of information about the expression profile of DUOX1/2 as well as their role in CVD."
                - paragraph [ref=e1462]:
                  - strong [ref=e1463]: Myeloperoxidase (MPO)
                  - text: — It is a tetrameric, heavily glycosylated and heme-containing protein which is a member of the heme peroxidase superfamily. The mature enzyme consists of two identical disulfide-linked dimers each of which possesses light and heavy chains [
                  - link "120" [ref=e1464] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib120"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "121" [ref=e1465] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib121"
                  - text: "]. MPO contained in azurophilic granules is released from stimulated phagocytes ["
                  - link "122" [ref=e1466] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib122"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "123" [ref=e1467] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib123"
                  - text: "] and uniquely can convert chlorides into hypochlorous acids that further involves in the generation of other ROS such as OH"
                  - superscript [ref=e1468]: •
                  - text: and NO
                  - subscript [ref=e1469]: "2"
                  - text: Cl [
                  - link "122" [ref=e1470] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib122"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "124" [ref=e1471] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib124"
                  - text: "]. Roles of MPO mainly focus on the tyrosine chlorination, pathogen killing, and the endothelial dysfunction in the vasculature ["
                  - link "125" [ref=e1472] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib125"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "126" [ref=e1473] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib126"
                  - text: "], which are related to actions of its catalytic activity and relevant downstream products. MPO also was identified as a NO oxidase to regulate NO bioavailability in which NO was catalytically consumed by radicals generated by MPO ["
                  - link "127" [ref=e1474] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib127"
                  - text: "]. Notably, MPO independent of its catalytic properties can cause a collapse of the endothelial glycocalyx via physically interacting with heparan sulfate side chains ["
                  - link "128" [ref=e1475] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib128"
                  - text: "]."
              - generic [ref=e1476]:
                - heading "6. Metabolic interplay between ROS generators (Fig. 2)" [level=2] [ref=e1477]:
                  - text: 6. Metabolic interplay between ROS generators (
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1478] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig2"
                  - text: )
                - paragraph [ref=e1479]:
                  - text: In most cases, cellular oxidative stress involves concomitant activation of multiple ROS generators. The cross talk between the major ROS generators (Mitochondria-ETC, NOX and NOS/XOR) could lead to further activation of each other, and result in positive feedback (a vicious cycle) which augments the ROS generation and oxidative stress [
                  - link "129" [ref=e1480] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib129"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "130" [ref=e1481] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib130"
                  - text: "]. We define the metabolic interplay or cross-talk between ROS generators as three pathways, 1) NOX-ROS promoted Mitochondria-ROS generation, 2) Mitochondria-ROS activated NOX-ROS generation, and 3) NOX/Mitochondria-ROS regulated eNOS/XR-ROS."
                - paragraph [ref=e1482]:
                  - strong [ref=e1483]: NOX-ROS promoted Mitochondria-ROS generation
                  - text: — a well-described NOX activation signaling is AngII stimulation. AngII binds to its receptor AT1R leading to the activation of NOX1/2 located in PM/ER/endosome and O
                  - subscript [ref=e1484]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1485]: •-
                  - text: generation [
                  - link "131" [ref=e1486] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib131"
                  - text: "]. Extracellular O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1487]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1488]: •-
                  - text: generated by PM-located NOX1/2 can be dismutated by SOD3 to form H
                  - subscript [ref=e1489]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1490]: "2"
                  - text: which then diffuses into the cytosol via water channel aquaporin. While intracellular O
                  - subscript [ref=e1491]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1492]: •-
                  - text: generated from ER/ES-located NOX1/2 can be dismutated by SOD1 to form H
                  - subscript [ref=e1493]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1494]: "2"
                  - text: which activates PKC-ε/Mitochondria-KATP signaling [
                  - link "132" [ref=e1495] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib132"
                  - text: "], leading to the increase of K"
                  - superscript [ref=e1496]: +
                  - text: influx, decrease of mitochondrial membrane potential (ΔΨm), inhibition of mitochondrial Com I and finally the increase of mitochondrial O
                  - subscript [ref=e1497]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1498]: •-
                  - text: production [
                  - link "133" [ref=e1499] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib133"
                  - text: "]("
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1500] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig2"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1501]: pathway 1a)
                  - text: . Mitochondria-ROS generation can be prevented by blocking the NOX1/2 or mitochondrial K
                  - subscript [ref=e1502]: ATP
                  - text: "["
                  - link "134" [ref=e1503] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib134"
                  - text: "], or by inhibiting NOX1/2 or PKCɛ activity ["
                  - link "135" [ref=e1504] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib135"
                  - text: "]. Mitochondria-ROS can also be produced by NOX4 in mitochondrial intermembrane in certain cell types such as CM and EC which can be activated by AngII/Gαq signaling ["
                  - link "136" [ref=e1505] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib136"
                  - text: "]("
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1506] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig2"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1507]: pathway 1b)
                  - text: . In addition, NOX-ROS can be generated by stimulation of advanced glycation end-products, lipopolysaccharide or cytokines, and results in Mitochondria-ROS production in rat renal cells [
                  - link "137" [ref=e1508] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib137"
                  - text: "] or glioma cells respectively ["
                  - link "138" [ref=e1509] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib138"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e1510]:
                  - strong [ref=e1511]: Mitochondria-ROS activated NOX-ROS
                  - text: — Mitochondria-ROS generated by Com I and NOX4 is mainly released to the mitochondrial matrix and dismutated by SOD2 to H
                  - subscript [ref=e1512]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1513]: "2"
                  - text: ", while O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1514]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1515]: •-
                  - text: generated by Com III can be released to either mitochondrial matrix or intermembrane space and dismutated by SOD1. A small amount of mitochondrial O
                  - subscript [ref=e1516]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1517]: •-
                  - text: can be transported to the cytosol by voltage-dependent anion channel (VDAC) directly and then dismutated by SOD1 to form H
                  - subscript [ref=e1518]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1519]: "2"
                  - text: . Mitochondria-generated H
                  - subscript [ref=e1520]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1521]: "2"
                  - text: can be leaked to the cytosol through water channel aquaporin (AQP). Mitochondria-derived H
                  - subscript [ref=e1522]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1523]: "2"
                  - text: then further activates NOX [
                  - link "134" [ref=e1524] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib134"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "139" [ref=e1525] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib139"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "140" [ref=e1526] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib140"
                  - text: "] by two pathways: PKC/cSrc activation and p47phox phosphorylation ["
                  - link "135" [ref=e1527] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib135"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "141" [ref=e1528] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib141"
                  - text: "]"
                  - strong [ref=e1529]: (
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1530] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig2"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1531]: pathway 2a)
                  - text: and cSrc mediated P22phox/NOX1/2/4 phosphorylation [
                  - link "142" [ref=e1532] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib142"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "143" [ref=e1533] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib143"
                  - text: "] ("
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1534] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig2"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1535]: pathway 2b)
                  - text: . Mitochondria-ROS was found responsible for NOX1/2 activation [
                  - link "[144]" [ref=e1536] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib144"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[145]" [ref=e1537] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib145"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[146]" [ref=e1538] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib146"
                  - text: "] and NOX1/2/4 induction ["
                  - link "142" [ref=e1539] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib142"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "143" [ref=e1540] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib143"
                  - text: "] as mitochondrial TEMPO can significantly attenuate these effect. NOX activity can be regulated by expression of mitochondrial-specific SOD2 which modulates AngII-enhanced mitochondrial ROS ["
                  - link "147" [ref=e1541] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib147"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e1542]:
                  - strong [ref=e1543]: NOX/Mitochondria-ROS regulated eNOS/XR-ROS
                  - text: — It is reported that oxidative stress is a major priming event for NOS uncoupling or XR to XO transformation. Cytosolic O
                  - subscript [ref=e1544]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1545]: •-
                  - text: from mitochondria or NOX can react with NO to form ONOO
                  - superscript [ref=e1546]: −
                  - text: ", resulting in eNOS uncoupling via depletion of BH4 or oxidative disruption of the zinc-sulfur complex, or XR to XO transformation via oxidation of critical thiols in Cys. XO and eNOS uncoupling further produce cytosolic O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1547]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1548]: •-
                  - text: and exacerbate intracellular oxidative stress (
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e1549] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig2"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1550]: pathway 3)
                  - text: . O
                  - subscript [ref=e1551]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1552]: •-
                  - text: would be incorporated into pathway 1a to be dismutated by SOD1 to form H
                  - subscript [ref=e1553]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1554]: "2"
                  - text: which initiate or enhance PKC-ε/Mitochondria-KATP. p47phox knockout abolished iNOS induction due to the significant reduction of the O
                  - subscript [ref=e1555]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1556]: •-
                  - text: production [
                  - link "148" [ref=e1557] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib148"
                  - text: "]. Inhibition of eNOS using l-NAME resulted in O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1558]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1559]: •-
                  - text: reduction [
                  - link "149" [ref=e1560] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib149"
                  - text: "]. It is reported that NOX inhibition prevents XR to XO transformation and further prevented O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1561]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1562]: •-
                  - text: production in endothelial cells under the oscillatory shear stress [
                  - link "150" [ref=e1563] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib150"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e1564]: Taken together, the interplay or cross-talk between mitochondrial ETC, NOX and NOS/XOR provide a network of intracellular redox regulation. Since NOX family members are the key members of this metabolic interplay, have convenient accessibility and are highly regulable, it seems the compatible target for intervention of the vicious oxidative stress circle.
              - generic [ref=e1565]:
                - heading "7. Phenotypes of ROS-related gene knockout in mice (Table 4)" [level=2] [ref=e1566]:
                  - text: 7. Phenotypes of ROS-related gene knockout in mice (
                  - link "Table 4" [ref=e1567] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tbl4"
                  - text: )
                - generic [ref=e1568]:

                        - cell [ref=e1610]

                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1771] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/tbl4/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1774]:
                    - text: The table summarizes phenotypes of mouse gene knockout associated with anti-oxidants, ROS generators, and other ROS regulators.
                    - strong [ref=e1775]: Abbreviation
                    - text: ": CAT, catalase; DUOX, Dual oxidase; DUOXA, Dual oxidase activator; eNOS, Endothelial nitric oxide synthase; GPX, glutathione peroxidase; H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1776]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1777]: "2"
                    - text: ", hydrogen peroxide; iNOS, inducible nitric oxide synthase; NA, Not available; nNOS, Neuronal nitric oxide synthase; NOX3, NADPH oxidase complex 3; NOXO1, NADPH oxidase organizer 1; NRF2, Nuclear Factor-Erythroid 2-Related Factor; O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1778]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1779]: •-
                    - text: ", Superoxide radical anion; PRDX, Peroxiredoxin; ROS, Reactive oxygen species; SOD, Superoxide dismutase; UCP3, Uncoupling protein 3; VDAC3, Voltage dependent anion channel type 3; XOR, xanthine oxidoreductase."
                - paragraph [ref=e1780]:
                  - link "Table 4" [ref=e1781] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tbl4"
                  - text: summarizes phenotypes of ROS relevant gene knockouts involving with ROS-scavenging/producing enzymes or other ROS regulators. Detrimental consequence occurs with either excessive or deficient ROS generation. SOD2 deficiency in mice is lethal with excessive ROS generation whereas a deficiency in SOD1 and SOD3 is not but with multiple pathological changes or various diseases [
                  - link "151" [ref=e1782] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib151"
                  - text: "]. Additionally, deficiency of Nox/Duox-derived O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1783]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1784]: •-
                  - text: and H
                  - subscript [ref=e1785]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1786]: "2"
                  - text: also would lead to diverse phenotypes such as hypothyroidism, CGD-like immune defect and a balance disorder [
                  - link "[152]" [ref=e1787] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib152"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[153]" [ref=e1788] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib153"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "[154]" [ref=e1789] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib154"
                  - text: "]. Voltage-dependent anion channels (VDACs) is a mitochondrial porin which controls the release of O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1790]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1791]: •-
                  - text: from mitochondria. Knockout of VDAC3 increases ROS levels and alters renal sodium transport, leading to hypertension [
                  - link "155" [ref=e1792] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib155"
                  - text: "]."
              - generic [ref=e1793]:
                - heading "8. ROS homeostasis and functional implications" [level=2] [ref=e1794]: 8. ROS homeostasis and functional implications
                - paragraph [ref=e1795]:
                  - text: (
                  - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e1796] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig3"
                  - text: )Cellular ROS levels undergo consistent changes in redox status. It is recognized that ROS can be signaling molecules and have essential physiological functions at basal levels. ROS is a double edged sword which can also result in oxidative stress.
                - figure [ref=e1797]:
                  - heading "Fig. 3." [level=3] [ref=e1798]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1799]:
                    - link "Fig. 3 🔍" [ref=e1800] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=6831867_gr3.jpg
                      - img "Fig. 3" [ref=e1801]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1803] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/fig3/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1805]:
                    - text: Cellular ROS homeostasis and their pathophysiological effects. Cellular ROS levels undergo consistent changes in redox status. In physiological condition, ROS is maintained at equilibrium levels to facilitate physiological redox signaling as described in the green radial network on the left. Impaired ROS production causes low redox status and suppresses physiological redox signaling. In the case of high ROS status or oxidative stress, excessive ROS would initiate pathological redox signaling and induce cellular damage and various diseases as indicated in the red radial network on the right. Type 1 ROS (O
                    - subscript [ref=e1806]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1807]: •-
                    - text: ", NO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1808]: •
                    - text: ", H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1809]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1810]: "2"
                    - text: ) is firstly generated and has essential physiological functions. Type 2 ROS (ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e1811]: −
                    - text: ", ONOOH, OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e1812]: •
                    - text: ", HOCl) and type 3 ROS (NO2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1813]: •
                    - text: ", CO3"
                    - superscript [ref=e1814]: •-
                    - text: ", RO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1815]: •
                    - text: /ROO
                    - superscript [ref=e1816]: •
                    - text: ) are subsequently products of Type 1 ROS and play important role in oxidative stress. ROS reactivity is defined based on descriptions in
                    - link "Table 1" [ref=e1817] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tbl1"
                    - text: "legend. Abbreviation: CO3"
                    - superscript [ref=e1818]: •-
                    - text: ", Carbonate radical anion; COPD, Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease; EC, Endothelial cells; HF, Heart failure; OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e1819]: •
                    - text: ", Hydroxyl radical; H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1820]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1821]: "2"
                    - text: ", Hydrogen peroxide; MC, Monocytes; MI, Myocardial infarction; NO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1822]: •
                    - text: ", Nitric oxide radical; NO"
                    - subscript [ref=e1823]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1824]: •
                    - text: ", Nitrogen dioxide radical; O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1825]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1826]: •-
                    - text: ", Superoxide radical anion; ONOO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1827]: −
                    - text: ", Peroxynitrite; RO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1828]: •
                    - text: ", Alkoxyl radical; ROO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1829]: •
                    - text: ", Peroxyl radical; ROS, Reactive oxygen species; VSMC, Vascular smooth muscle cells."
                - paragraph [ref=e1830]:
                  - text: In physiological condition, ROS is maintained at equilibrium levels to facilitate physiological redox signaling. Normal level of type 1 ROS is required for cell metabolism [
                  - link "156" [ref=e1831] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib156"
                  - text: "], survival ["
                  - link "157" [ref=e1832] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib157"
                  - text: "], proliferation and differentiation ["
                  - link "158" [ref=e1833] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib158"
                  - text: "], angiogenesis ["
                  - link "159" [ref=e1834] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib159"
                  - text: "], neurogenesis ["
                  - link "160" [ref=e1835] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib160"
                  - text: "], hematopoiesis ["
                  - link "161" [ref=e1836] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib161"
                  - text: "], morphogenesis ["
                  - link "162" [ref=e1837] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib162"
                  - text: "], immune defense ["
                  - link "163" [ref=e1838] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib163"
                  - text: "], etc ("
                  - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e1839] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig3"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1840]: green radial network
                  - text: ). It needed to be noticed that the normal level of ROS differs in cell types. Proliferation cells like progenitor cells usually have a higher basal level of ROS. The effect of ROS/RNS also depends on the duration of exposure. But impaired ROS production causes low redox status and suppresses physiological redox signaling.
                - paragraph [ref=e1841]:
                  - text: In the case of high ROS status or oxidative stress, excessive ROS would initiate pathological redox signaling and induce cellular damage and various disease. In pathological conditions when cellular ROS/RNS generation shifts to more toxic ROS/RNS species of type 2 or 3, oxidative stress can be induced. For instance, in a disease with iron accumulation, H
                  - subscript [ref=e1842]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1843]: "2"
                  - text: can be converted to the extremely toxic OH
                  - superscript [ref=e1844]: •
                  - text: via the Haber-Weiss reaction (also called Fenton reaction). Even mild elevation of these species within the cells will cause oxidative stress and cytotoxicity. Higher level of ROS causes stem cell premature exhaustion [
                  - link "164" [ref=e1845] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib164"
                  - text: "], tumorigenesis ["
                  - link "165" [ref=e1846] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib165"
                  - text: "], autoimmunity ["
                  - link "166" [ref=e1847] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib166"
                  - text: "], inflammation ["
                  - link "167" [ref=e1848] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib167"
                  - text: "], EC dysfunction ["
                  - link "168" [ref=e1849] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib168"
                  - text: "], VSMC proliferation ["
                  - link "169" [ref=e1850] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib169"
                  - text: "], multiple sclerosis ["
                  - link "170" [ref=e1851] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib170"
                  - text: "], cell death ["
                  - link "171" [ref=e1852] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib171"
                  - text: "], etc ("
                  - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e1853] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#fig3"
                  - text: ","
                  - strong [ref=e1854]: red radial network
                  - text: ).
                - paragraph [ref=e1855]:
                  - text: The functional implication of ROS is determined by their inherent chemical features, including cellular concentration, time of exposure, and compartmentation [
                  - link "172" [ref=e1856] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib172"
                  - text: "]. Based on their functional implication, reactivity, and target selectivity, we classified ROS into three types as summarized in"
                  - link "Table 5" [ref=e1857] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#tbl5"
                  - text: .
                - generic [ref=e1858]:
                  - heading "Table 5." [level=3] [ref=e1859]
                  - paragraph [ref=e1861]: Features of three ROS types.
                  - table [ref=e1863]:

                          - text: ", nitrosyl-metal complex formation"
                      - row "Type 2 ROS OH•, HOCl, ONOO− /ONOOH Secondary High (≥++++) Indiscriminately reacting with DNA, lipids, and proteins" [ref=e1887]:
                        - cell "Type 2 ROS" [ref=e1888]

                          - text: OH

                        - cell "Secondary" [ref=e1892]

                        - cell "Indiscriminately reacting with DNA, lipids, and proteins" [ref=e1894]
                      - row "Type 3 ROS NO2•, CO3•-, RO• /ROO• Tertiary Mediate (+++) /high Reacting with most of biomolecules leading to deteriorating oxidative stress" [ref=e1895]:
                        - cell "Type 3 ROS" [ref=e1896]
                        - cell "NO2•, CO3•-, RO• /ROO•" [ref=e1897]:
                          - text: "NO"
                          - subscript [ref=e1898]: "2"
                          - superscript [ref=e1899]: •
                          - text: ", CO"
                          - subscript [ref=e1900]: "3"
                          - superscript [ref=e1901]: •-
                          - text: ","
