                          - superscript [ref=e1902]: •
                          - text: /ROO
                          - superscript [ref=e1903]: •
                        - cell "Tertiary" [ref=e1904]
                        - cell "Mediate (+++) /high" [ref=e1905]
                        - cell "Reacting with most of biomolecules leading to deteriorating oxidative stress" [ref=e1906]
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1908] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/tbl5/
                  - paragraph [ref=e1911]:
                    - text: Type 1 ROS (O
                    - subscript [ref=e1912]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1913]: •-
                    - text: ", NO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1914]: •
                    - text: ", H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1915]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1916]: "2"
                    - text: ) is firstly generated and has essential physiological functions. Type 2 ROS (ONOO
                    - superscript [ref=e1917]: −
                    - text: ", ONOOH, OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e1918]: •
                    - text: ", HOCl) and type 3 ROS (NO"
                    - subscript [ref=e1919]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1920]: •
                    - text: ", CO"
                    - subscript [ref=e1921]: "3"
                    - superscript [ref=e1922]: •-
                    - text: ", RO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1923]: •
                    - text: /ROO
                    - superscript [ref=e1924]: •
                    - text: ) are subsequently products of Type 1 ROS and play important role in oxidative stress. ROS reactivity is defined based on descriptions in
                    - link "Table 1" [ref=e1925] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#tbl1"
                    - text: legend.
                    - strong [ref=e1926]: Abbreviation
                    - text: ": CO3"
                    - superscript [ref=e1927]: •-
                    - text: ", Carbonate radical anion; OH"
                    - superscript [ref=e1928]: •
                    - text: ", Hydroxyl radical; H"
                    - subscript [ref=e1929]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e1930]: "2"
                    - text: ", Hydrogen peroxide; NO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1931]: •
                    - text: ", Nitric oxide radical; NO"
                    - subscript [ref=e1932]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1933]: •
                    - text: ", Nitrogen dioxide radical; O"
                    - subscript [ref=e1934]: "2"
                    - superscript [ref=e1935]: •-
                    - text: ", Superoxide radical anion; ONOO-, Peroxynitrite; RO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1936]: •
                    - text: ", Alkoxyl radical; ROO"
                    - superscript [ref=e1937]: •
                    - text: ", Peroxyl radical; ROS, Reactive oxygen species."
                - paragraph [ref=e1938]:
                  - strong [ref=e1939]: Type 1 ROS
                  - text: — Type 1 ROS are firstly generated and have essential physiological functions, including O
                  - subscript [ref=e1940]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1941]: •-
                  - text: ", NO"
                  - superscript [ref=e1942]: •
                  - text: and H
                  - subscript [ref=e1943]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1944]: "2"
                  - text: . Type 1 ROS have relatively low reactivity (+ and ++), are relatively selective to their molecular targets, and play both physiological and pathological roles. They are initial ROS species and serve as essential redox signaling molecules regulating physiological functions [
                  - link "173" [ref=e1945] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib173"
                  - text: "]. Nanomolar NO concentration can serve as an antioxidant and essential redox signaling molecules for vessel relaxation, thrombosis, and immune system hemostasis, whereas micromolar NO concentration will react with O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1946]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1947]: •-
                  - text: to generate cytotoxic peroxynitrite causing oxidative stress and cell injury [
                  - link "174" [ref=e1948] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib174"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "175" [ref=e1949] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib175"
                  - text: "]. Likewise, low level of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e1950]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1951]: "2"
                  - text: serves as an important mediator of growth factor-induced receptor tyrosine phosphorylation by inhibition of protein tyrosine phosphatases (PTPs) via oxidizing its Cys to reversible sulfenic form Cys–SOH, while high level H
                  - subscript [ref=e1952]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1953]: "2"
                  - text: can lead to damage of PTPs and hyperactive growth signaling via further oxidizing reversible Cys–SOH to irreversible form of sulfinic or sulfonic [
                  - link "173" [ref=e1954] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib173"
                  - text: "]. O"
                  - subscript [ref=e1955]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1956]: •-
                  - text: mediated the major epigenetic progress via DNA methylation, histone methylation, and histone acetylation, and also regulated autophagy [
                  - link "176" [ref=e1957] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib176"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "177" [ref=e1958] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib177"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e1959]:
                  - strong [ref=e1960]: Type 2 ROS
                  - text: — Type 2 ROS (ONOO
                  - superscript [ref=e1961]: −
                  - text: ", ONOOH, OH"
                  - superscript [ref=e1962]: •
                  - text: ", HOCl) are subsequent products of Type 1 ROS and play an important role in oxidative stress. Type 2 ROS are mostly pathological relevant, have relatively high reactivity (≥+++), and low selectivity to their molecular targets. Hydroxyl radical can indiscriminately react with its targets resulting in lesions or instability of DNA ["
                  - link "178" [ref=e1963] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib178"
                  - text: "]. ONOO"
                  - superscript [ref=e1964]: −
                  - text: /ONOOH can oxidize or nitrate a wide range of macromolecules. HOCl produced by MPO in neutrophil is involved in oxidization or chlorination of many biological molecules such as thiols, cholesterol, and unsaturated lipid, DNA, ascorbate and urate [
                  - link "54" [ref=e1965] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib54"
                  - text: "]. When they are generated in the peroxisome, they are efficient tools for bacteria killing. However, within another intercellular organelle, they can cause irreversible oxidation of most biomolecules. Only when ROS generation overwhelms the antioxidant defense, they cause oxidative stress and cellular damages."
                - paragraph [ref=e1966]:
                  - strong [ref=e1967]: Type 3 ROS
                  - text: — Type 3 ROS (NO
                  - subscript [ref=e1968]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1969]: •
                  - text: ", CO"
                  - subscript [ref=e1970]: "3"
                  - superscript [ref=e1971]: •-
                  - text: ", RO"
                  - superscript [ref=e1972]: •
                  - text: /ROO
                  - superscript [ref=e1973]: •
                  - text: ) are also subsequent products of Type 1 ROS and powerful oxidative stress inducer. Their reactivity is mediate or high but selectivity is relatively low for oxidizing substrates. Type 3 ROS form the downstream metabolites of type 2 ROS and react with most of the biomolecules leading to cellular damages and deteriorating oxidative stress. For instance, peroxynitrite-mediated protein tyrosine nitration is further promoted by type 3 ROS of NO
                  - subscript [ref=e1974]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1975]: •
                  - text: ", CO"
                  - subscript [ref=e1976]: "3"
                  - superscript [ref=e1977]: •-
                  - text: and lipid peroxyl radicals arising from peroxynitrite [
                  - link "45" [ref=e1978] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib45"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e1979]:
                  - strong [ref=e1980]: ROS compartmentation —
                  - text: ROS metabolism has strong intracellular compartmentation. The redox code recently comes out as a methodology to study the spatiotemporal organization of redox systems, especially for activation/deactivation cycles involving O
                  - subscript [ref=e1981]: "2"
                  - text: and H
                  - subscript [ref=e1982]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1983]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e1984]: "26"
                  - text: . ROS are usually accumulated in a certain cellular compartment, where the antioxidant surrounding ROS generator therefore quickly remove ROS and prevent its diffusion. For example, H
                  - subscript [ref=e1985]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1986]: "2"
                  - text: is able to diffuse throughout the cell and react with its target. However, H
                  - subscript [ref=e1987]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1988]: "2"
                  - text: generated from NADPH oxidase NOX4 in the ER only oxidize protein tyrosine phosphatase 1B located nearby NOX4 [
                  - link "179" [ref=e1989] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib179"
                  - text: "]. Oxidation of cysteine residue by H"
                  - subscript [ref=e1990]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1991]: "2"
                  - text: within the ER compartment induces disulfide bond formation which is required for protein folding and secretion. Additionally, H
                  - subscript [ref=e1992]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1993]: "2"
                  - text: was formed earlier in mitochondria following monoamine oxidase activation as compared to the cytosol [
                  - link "172" [ref=e1994] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib172"
                  - text: "]. The spatiotemporal manner of ROS generation indicates that the mitochondria is the initial organelle in which endogenous oxidative stress occurs. Subsequent signaling cascades in other compartments would be triggered by mitochondrial ROS generations. Superoxide generated from cytosol has outcome difference with that in the mitochondrial matrix in which the high content of iron-sulfur cluster exist ["
                  - link "173" [ref=e1995] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib173"
                  - text: "]. Oxidation occurring in the cytosol is involved with translocation of proteins to other compartments such as nuclei for their signal transduction. Cysteine residues of transcription factors need to remain the reduced and functional status to maintain zinc-finger DNA binding motifs, in which GSH and Trx 1 play a pivotal role in the nuclei compartment ["
                  - link "172" [ref=e1996] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib172"
                  - text: "]. ROS generation is also heterogeneous and varies in different microdomains of cellular organelles in response to different stimuli ["
                  - link "172" [ref=e1997] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib172"
                  - text: "]. For instance, H"
                  - subscript [ref=e1998]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e1999]: "2"
                  - text: would be formed in the endosomes and the ER membrane under activation of the epidermal growth factor receptor, but would be formed at the plasma membrane with stimulation of platelet-derived growth factor receptor. Different NOX component can be activated in the specific compartment which account for the differential ROS formation [
                  - link "172" [ref=e2000] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#bib172"
                  - text: "]. Obviously, ROS compartmentation contributes to its functional specificity."
              - generic [ref=e2001]:
                - heading "9. Concluding remarks" [level=2] [ref=e2002]: 9. Concluding remarks
                - paragraph [ref=e2003]: As highlighted in this review, it is particularly vital to understand major pathways of ROS generation and clearance, the interplaying network between various ROS generators, and roles of each ROS type in cellular redox biology and oxidative stress. The normal level of ROS is required for specific signaling transduction in normal cell function and survival, which maintains redox homeostasis. However, excess ROS generation would disturb the physiological function of redox balance, inducing oxidative stress and resulting in the cell or tissue damages. ROS serves as critical mediators in cell signaling regulation for both physiological and pathophysiological status. This article provides intensive knowledge about the biochemical basis of redox regulation and metabolic interplay, and important guidance for future studies involving redox modification.
